**2.1.1**  Vaiśampāyana said: Then Maya spoke to Pārtha in the presence of Vāsudeva, with joined palms, honouring him again and again with gentle words.

**2.1.2**  "By you I have been saved from this wrathful Kṛṣṇa and from Pāvaka who sought to burn me. O son of Kuntī, tell me — what shall I do for you?"

**2.1.3**  Arjuna said: "You have already done everything. Go in peace, O great asura. Be ever well-disposed toward me, and we too shall be well-disposed toward you."

**2.1.4**  Maya said: "This is fitting in you, O lord, O bull among men, just as you have said. Out of affection I still wish to do something for you, O Bhārata."

**2.1.5**  "For I am the Viśvakarmā of the dānavas, their great architect. I therefore wish to do something on your behalf, O Pāṇḍava."

**2.1.6**  Arjuna said: "You consider yourself delivered from mortal peril by me. Given this, I am unable to make you do anything for me."

**2.1.7**  "Nor do I wish your resolve to go in vain, O dānava. Let something be done for Kṛṣṇa in the same way as has been done for me."

**2.1.8**  Vaiśampāyana said: Thus prompted by Maya, O bull of the Bharatas, Vāsudeva reflected for a moment on what might be asked of him.

**2.1.9**  Kṛṣṇa then urged him: "Let a hall be built, O Daiteya, for the king Dharmarāja — such a one as you deem fitting here."

**2.1.10**  "Build such an assembly hall that no human, seeing it, would be able to replicate it, and all men in this mortal world would be amazed."

**2.1.11**  "Where we might behold divine designs arranged by you — both asura and human alike — build such a hall, O Maya."

**2.1.12**  Accepting those words with great delight, Maya then joyfully built the assembly hall for the Pāṇḍava, resembling a celestial palace.

**2.1.13**  Then Kṛṣṇa and Pārtha duly reported all of this to King Yudhiṣṭhira, the Dharmarāja, and presented Maya to him.

**2.1.14**  Yudhiṣṭhira then honoured Maya fittingly, and Maya, himself well honoured, accepted that honour with respect.

**2.1.15**  That Daiteya then recounted here and there the ancient deeds of the gods to the sons of Pāṇḍu, O lord of the people, O Bhārata.

**2.1.16**  Having rested for some time and deliberated, that Viśvakarmā set about building the assembly hall for the great-souled Pāṇḍavas.

**2.1.17**  In accordance with the wishes of the Pārthas and the great-souled Kṛṣṇa, on an auspicious day that greatly radiant one performed the auspicious rites with festive intent.

**2.1.18**  That mighty one satisfied thousands of the foremost twice-born with rice-milk porridge, and gave them also manifold wealth.

**2.1.19**  He measured out on all sides that hall — endowed with the qualities of all seasons, divine in form, enchanting to the mind — at ten thousand kiṣku in extent.

**2.2.1**  Vaiśampāyana said: Janārdana, worthy of worship and duly worshipped, had dwelt in pleasant residence at Khāṇḍavaprastha together with the Pārthas, united in affection.

**2.2.2**  That broad-eyed one, longing to see his father, resolved to depart. He then took leave of Dharmarāja and of Pṛthā.

**2.2.3**  He who is worshipped by the world bowed his head to the feet of his father's sister; she smelled the top of his head and embraced Keśava.

**2.2.4**  Then the greatly glorious Kṛṣṇa saw his own sister. Approaching her, Hṛṣīkeśa went to her filled with affection and tears.

**2.2.5**  The blessed lord spoke to the auspicious Subhadrā, that sweet-spoken one, words that were meaningful, true, beneficial, brief, apt, and unsurpassed.

**2.2.6**  By her he was made to hear words conveying news of their kinsmen; she honoured him repeatedly and paid obeisance to him with bowed head.

**2.2.7**  Having taken leave of that spirited lady and received her blessing, Janārdana the Vārṣṇeya then saw Kṛṣṇā and Dhaumya as well.

**2.2.8**  That best of men bowed to Dhaumya in the proper manner; and having comforted Draupadī and taken his leave, Janārdana

**2.2.9**  went, wise and mighty, to meet the brothers, accompanied by Pārtha. Kṛṣṇa, surrounded by the five brothers, shone like Śakra among the immortals.

**2.2.10**  That bull of the Yadus worshipped both gods and twice-born with garlands, recitations, prostrations, and fragrances of various kinds. Having performed all necessary rites, that foremost among the steadfast set out.

**2.2.11**  He, worthy of auspicious recitations, had the brāhmaṇas intone svasti with bowls of curds, fruits, and unbroken rice; then, having given them wealth, he circumambulated.

**2.2.12**  He mounted his golden chariot bearing the banner of Tārkṣya, swift as the wind, furnished with mace, discus, sword, Śārṅga bow, and other weapons.

**2.2.13**  On an auspicious lunar day, under a favourable asterism and in a propitious muhūrta, the lotus-eyed one departed, his horses Śainy and Sugrīva drawing the chariot.

**2.2.14**  King Yudhiṣṭhira also mounted that chariot out of affection; and setting aside Dāruka, his excellent charioteer, the lord of the Kurus himself then took up the reins.

**2.2.15**  Arjuna too mounted and waved over his head a large white fly-whisk with a golden handle, turning it in a clockwise direction.

**2.2.16**  Likewise Bhīmasena, self-restrained and accompanied by the twins, followed behind Kṛṣṇa, along with the officiating priests and the townspeople.

**2.2.17**  Keśava, slayer of enemy heroes, being followed in this manner by the brothers, shone resplendent like a beloved teacher followed by his devoted pupils.

**2.2.18**  Govinda took leave of Pārtha and embraced him tightly; he honoured Yudhiṣṭhira, and Bhīmasena and the twins likewise.

**2.2.19**  Tightly embraced by the two twins and saluted by them, Madhusūdana then made his farewells to all of them in the proper manner.

**2.2.20**  Having turned back the Pāṇḍavas together with their retinues, Kṛṣṇa departed for his own city, like a second Purandara.

**2.2.21**  They followed him with their eyes as far as the range of vision; then, out of love, they followed Kṛṣṇa with their minds.

**2.2.22**  Though their minds were unsated in beholding Keśava, the glorious Śauri, fair to behold, quickly vanished from their sight.

**2.2.23**  Those bulls among men, the Pārthas, their minds gone with Govinda and as though unwilling, all turned back and returned to their city. And Kṛṣṇa too in his chariot reached Dvārakā in due time.

**2.3.1**  Vaiśampāyana said: Then Maya said to Pārtha Arjuna, foremost among the victorious: "I take my leave of you; I shall go swiftly and shall return as well."

**2.3.2**  "When all the dānavas were about to perform a sacrifice, I made a jewel-adorned vessel, beautiful, near Bindu Lake, to the north of Kailāsa toward Mount Maināka."

**2.3.3**  "I shall come back bringing whatever was in the assembly hall of the truth-pledged Vṛṣaparvan — if it still remains there, O Bhārata."

**2.3.4**  "Then I shall build for the glorious Pāṇḍava an assembly hall delightful to the mind, wondrous, adorned with all manner of jewels."

**2.3.5**  "There at Bindu Lake, O foremost of the Kurus, lies the finest of maces, deposited there by King Yuvanāśva after slaying his foes in battle — decorated with golden spots, heavy, able to bear great weight, and firm."

**2.3.6**  "That mace is the equal of a hundred thousand in might, a slayer of all foes, and fitting for Bhīma as the Gāṇḍīva bow is fitting for you."

**2.3.7**  "And there is also the great conch Devadatta, of Varuṇa, with its magnificent sound. All of this I shall give you — there is no doubt of it." Having said this, that asura set out in the north-eastern direction from Pārtha.

**2.3.8**  To the north of Kailāsa, toward Mount Maināka, stands the holy mountain with golden peaks, great and studded with precious gems.

**2.3.9**  The beautiful lake called Bindu is there — where King Bhagīratha, having seen the Bhāgīrathī Gaṅgā, dwelt for many years.

**2.3.10**  It is where the great-souled Lord of all beings, having sacrificed, gathered a hundred foremost sacrificial rites, O best of the Bharatas.

**2.3.11**  There the sacrificial posts are made of jewels and the fire altars of gold — arranged there for beauty, not built as mere imitations of the usual pattern.

**2.3.12**  It is where the thousand-eyed Śacīpati, having sacrificed there, attained perfection; where the eternal Lord of all beings, the sharp-blazing one, having created all the worlds, is worshipped, surrounded by thousands of creatures.

**2.3.13**  Where Nara and Nārāyaṇa, Brahmā, Yama, and Sthāṇu as the fifth attend a sacrificial session at the turn of every thousand yugas.

**2.3.14**  Where Vāsudeva performed sacrificial sessions for thousands of years with constant faith, for the upholding of the code of the righteous.

**2.3.15**  Where Keśava set up sacrificial posts garlanded with gold and altars blazing brilliantly, and gave there thousands upon millions in gifts.

**2.3.16**  Going there he took up the mace and the conch, O Bhārata, and the crystal hall-material that had belonged to Vṛṣaparvan; together with his attendant demons he took all of it.

**2.3.17**  Having brought all that, the asura built that peerless assembly hall — celebrated in the three worlds, divine, made of jewels, and auspicious.

**2.3.18**  He then presented the foremost mace to Bhīmasena, and gave to Pārtha the incomparable conch Devadatta.

**2.3.19**  That hall, O great king, had pillars made of refined gold; it extended ten thousand kiṣku in every direction.

**2.3.20**  Blazing as fire, as the sun, and as the moon, that divine hall bore the most magnificent form.

**2.3.21**  As though rivalling with its radiance the brilliant lustre of the sun, it shone, blazing as if with a divine, celestial splendour.

**2.3.22**  Resembling a mountain or a cloud, it stood encompassing the sky — vast, broad, smooth, free from sin, free from fatigue.

**2.3.23**  Furnished with the finest materials, garlanded with walls of gems, rich in jewels and in wealth, beautifully built by Viśvakarmā.

**2.3.24**  Neither the Sudharmā of the Dāśārhas nor that of Brahma was endowed with beauty comparable to the peerless hall that Maya built.

**2.3.25**  There, as directed by Maya, eight thousand rākṣasas called kiṃkaras both guarded and served that hall.

**2.3.26**  Moving through the sky, terrible, huge of body, immensely powerful, with red eyes or tawny eyes, with ears like shells, and wielders of weapons.

**2.3.27**  Within that hall Maya made a matchless lotus pond, with lapis-lazuli leaves spread out and lotuses of crystal stems.

**2.3.28**  Filled with lotus flowers and water lilies, thronged with many kinds of birds, its beauty enhanced by blooming lotuses, and adorned with tortoises and fish.

**2.3.29**  With excellent access to its banks, unpolluted, holding water good in every season, auspicious, and sprinkled with pearl-drops tossed up by the wind alone.

**2.3.30**  Some kings who came near that floor, set with gems and jewels, even on seeing it did not recognise it for what it was, and out of ignorance fell into it.

**2.3.31**  Around that hall on all sides grew great trees always in flower, of various kinds, blue-green, with cool shade, enchanting to the mind.

**2.3.32**  Fragrant groves and lotus pools on every side, populated with geese and ducks, beautified by cakravāka birds.

**2.3.33**  The wind, bearing the fragrance of garlands of water-born and land-born flowers from every direction, fanned the Pāṇḍavas.

**2.3.34**  Having built such a hall and completed it within fourteen months, Maya presented it to the king Dharmarāja.

**2.4.1**  Vaiśampāyana said: Then King Yudhiṣṭhira made his formal entry into that hall; the lord of men fed ten thousand brāhmaṇas.

**2.4.2**  With clarified-butter porridge and honey, with sweetmeats and roots and fruits, and also with unwashed garments and garlands of every kind.

**2.4.3**  The lord gave each of them thousands of cows individually; there arose a cry of "puṇyāha!" that touched the very sky, O Bhārata.

**2.4.4**  With various musical instruments and songs, with fragrances of every kind, the foremost of the Kurus worshipped and installed the divinities.

**2.4.5**  There wrestlers, actors, jesters, bards, and panegyrists attended the great-souled Yudhishthira for seven nights.

**2.4.6**  Having thus performed that ceremony of honor, the Pandava together with his brothers took delight in that beautiful assembly hall, as Indra rejoices in heaven.

**2.4.7**  Sages were seated in that assembly hall together with the Pandavas, and kings who had come from many different lands also took their seats there.

**2.4.8**  Asita, Devala, Satya, Sarpamali, Mahasiras, Arvavasu, Sumitra, Maitreya, Sunaka, and Bali were among them.

**2.4.9**  Baka Dalbhya, Sthulasiras, Krishnadvapayana, Shuka, Sumantru, Jaimini, Paila — the disciples of Vyasa — and ourselves were likewise present.

**2.4.10**  Tittiri, Yajnavalkya with his son, Lomaharshana, Apsuhomya, Dhaumya, and also Animandavya and Kaushika were there.

**2.4.11**  Damoshniṣa, Traivaṇi, Parṇada, Ghaṭajanuka, Mauñjayana, Vayubhaksha, Parasharya, and the two known as Sarika were present.

**2.4.12**  Balavaka, Shinivaka, Sutyapala, Kritasrama, Jatukarṇa, Shikhavant, Subala, and Parijataka were also there.

**2.4.13**  The greatly blessed Parvata, the sage Markandeya, Pavitrapani, Savarni, Bhaluki, and likewise Galava were present.

**2.4.14**  Janghabandhu, Raibhya, Kopavegashrava, Bhrigu, Haribabhru, Kaundinia, Babhrumali, and Sanatana were there as well.

**2.4.15**  Kakshivan Aushija, Nachiketa, Gautama, Painga, Varaha, Shunaka, the great ascetic Shandilya, Karkara, Venujangha, Kalapa, and Katha were also among them.

**2.4.16**  These sages, virtuous and righteous, of firm minds and with senses subdued, and many others well-versed in the Vedas and their auxiliary branches, were gathered there.

**2.4.17**  The foremost of sages, knowing dharma, pure and spotless, attended upon the great-souled king in the assembly hall, narrating sacred stories.

**2.4.18**  Likewise the foremost of kshatriyas attended upon the king of dharma — the splendid, great-souled, dharma-minded Muñjaketu Vardhana.

**2.4.19**  Samgramajit, Durmukha, the mighty Ugrasena, Kakshena the lord of the earth, Kshemaka the unconquered, the king of the Kambojas, Kamala, and the greatly powerful Kampana were present.

**2.4.20**  He alone had constantly shaken the Yavanas, just as the god who wields the thunderbolt shakes the Kalakeya demons.

**2.4.21**  The king Jatasura and Madrakanta, Kunti, Kuniṇda, the king of the Kiratas, the rulers of Anga and Vanga together with the king of Puṇdra, and the kings of the Pandyas and Odras together with the king of the Andhras were there.

**2.4.22**  The king of the Kiratas named Sumana, the lord of the Yavanas, Chanura, Devarata, the Bhoja, and the one called Bhimaratha were there.

**2.4.23**  Shrutayudha the Kalinga, Jayatsena the Magadha, Susharmana, Chekitana, and Suratha the destroyer of foes were present.

**2.4.24**  Ketuman, Vasudana, the king of Videha, Kritakshana, Sudharmana, Aniruddha, and the mighty Shrutayu were also there.

**2.4.25**  The invincible king of the Anupa country, Kshemajit, Sudakshina, Shishupala with his hundred sons, and likewise the lord of the Karushas were present.

**2.4.26**  The invincible princes of the Vrisnis, god-like in appearance — Ahuka, Viprithu, Gada, and Sarana — were also there.

**2.4.27**  Akrura, Kritavarma, Satyaki the son of Shini, Bhishmaka, Ahriti, the mighty Dyumatsena, the great archers the Kekayas, and Yajnasena the son of Saumaka were present.

**2.4.28**  Taking refuge with Arjuna, mighty princes clad in deer and antelope hides learned the science of archery there.

**2.4.29**  There too, O king, were trained the princes of the Vrishni clan — the son of Rukmini, Samba, and Yuyudhana the son of Satyaki.

**2.4.30**  These and many other kings, O lord of the earth, were present there, and Tumburu, the friend of Dhananjaya, also always abided there.

**2.4.31**  Chitrasena, accompanied by his ministers, and the Gandharvas and Apsaras, skilled in song and instrumental music, expert in rhythm and beat, were also present.

**2.4.32**  Kimnaras accomplished in proper measure and in the right tempo, urged on by Tumburu, sang together with the Gandharvas.

**2.4.33**  Those noble beings sang with divine melodies according to proper rule, delighting and attending upon the sons of Pandu and the sages.

**2.4.34**  Seated in that assembly hall, those of good vows and true resolve attended upon Yudhishthira as the gods in heaven attend upon Brahma.

**2.5.1**  Vaishampayana said: While the great-souled Pandavas were seated there, O Bharata, and the great Gandharvas were likewise seated,

**2.5.2**  The sage Narada, of great brilliance, who roams through all the worlds, came to that assembly hall then, accompanied by sages.

**2.5.3**  The divine sage of boundless radiance came with Parijata, the wise Raivata, and the gentle Sumukha, moving swift as thought, joyfully desiring to see the Pandavas assembled in the hall, O king of kings.

**2.5.4**  Seeing the sage Narada arrive, the foremost of the Pandavas, who knew all dharma, quickly rose together with his younger brothers and greeted him with affection, bowing humbly at that time.

**2.5.5**  Offering him a seat worthy of him according to proper rites, the one who knew dharma honored him with jewels and with all things desired.

**2.5.6**  Honored by all the Pandavas, the great sage who had mastered the Vedas then questioned Yudhishthira on matters combining dharma, desire, and material welfare.

**2.5.7**  Narada said: Do your enterprises prosper? Does your mind delight in dharma? Do you experience pleasures, and is your mind not afflicted?

**2.5.8**  O lord of men, do you follow the inexhaustible way of conduct practiced by your forefathers and grandfathers — a way accompanied by both dharma and material welfare — among your people?

**2.5.9**  Do you not harm dharma by means of material interest, or material interest by means of dharma, or both of them by means of selfish desire, treating affection as paramount?

**2.5.10**  O best of the victorious, O giver of boons, knowing the proper time, do you always attend to material welfare, dharma, and desire by apportioning them in due season?

**2.5.11**  O sinless one, do you properly examine the six royal qualities, the seven means of policy, and likewise the fourteen factors of strength and weakness?

**2.5.12**  O best of victors, O Bharata, examining yourself and your opponents, and thus resolving upon actions, do you pursue the eight methods of statecraft?

**2.5.13**  O bull of the Bharatas, have your six constituent elements not become diminished — are they prosperous, free from vice, and completely devoted to you?

**2.5.14**  Is your counsel never disclosed through inferences, through envoys, or through those who seem above suspicion — whether it leaks from you or from your ministers?

**2.5.15**  Do you pursue peace and war at the proper time? Do you follow the right conduct toward the neutral and the mediating power?

**2.5.16**  O hero, have you appointed as your ministers men who are your equals in intellect, pure, able to sustain their lives, of noble birth, and devoted to you?

**2.5.17**  For victory of kings, O Bharata, is rooted in counsel, well concealed by ministers rich in counsel and versed in the scriptures.

**2.5.18**  Do you not fall under the power of sleep? Do you wake at the proper time? O knower of purpose, do you also reflect on your aims in the latter part of the night?

**2.5.19**  Do you not deliberate alone? Do you also not deliberate with too many? Does the counsel you have deliberated upon not run abroad through the kingdom?

**2.5.20**  Having determined upon purposes that are small in their beginnings but great in their fruits, do you quickly set about to accomplish them and not obstruct such plans?

**2.5.21**  Are not all your affairs out of sight and in doubt, or all of them abandoned? For a mixed approach is the cause of success here.

**2.5.22**  O king, O hero, do your men know which tasks have been accomplished and which are nearly complete, and are there none remaining unaccomplished?

**2.5.23**  Are all your administrators, versed in all the scriptures, training the princes and the leading warriors in every way?

**2.5.24**  Do you purchase one learned man at the price of a thousand fools? For a learned man can bring about the highest good in times of difficulty in one's affairs.

**2.5.25**  Are all your fortresses fully stocked with wealth, grain, weapons, water, engines of war, craftsmen, and archers?

**2.5.26**  Even one intelligent, heroic, self-restrained, and discerning minister can bring a king or a prince to great prosperity.

**2.5.27**  Do you, through three sets of three spies unknown to one another, keep track of the eighteen key positions in the enemy's camp, and the fifteen in your own, using your scouts?

**2.5.28**  O destroyer of enemies, unknown to your foes and always prepared, do you constantly and vigilantly watch all your enemies?

**2.5.29**  Is your royal priest a man of good conduct, of noble birth, widely learned, free from envy, not given to questioning others, and honored by you?

**2.5.30**  Is the one appointed over your sacred fires a man who knows the rites, is intelligent, upright, and who always duly reports what has been offered and what is about to be offered at the proper time?

**2.5.31**  Is your astrologer well-versed in the auxiliary branches of the Vedas, expert in astronomy, and skilled in interpreting all kinds of portents?

**2.5.32**  Are the principal servants employed in principal tasks, the middling in middling tasks, and the lowest in the lowest tasks?

**2.5.33**  Do you appoint in the highest tasks ministers who have been tested by the fourfold test, whose loyalty was also that of their fathers and grandfathers, who are pure and excellent?

**2.5.34**  O bull of the Bharatas, do your ministers not govern the kingdom in such a way that the subjects are greatly troubled by excessively harsh punishment?

**2.5.35**  Do the officiating priests not disregard you as they would a fallen man, or as women disregard one who is excessively demanding in receiving gifts and is driven by desire?

**2.5.36**  Is your commander of the army bold, heroic, intelligent, steadfast, pure, of noble birth, devoted to you, and capable?

**2.5.37**  Are all the chiefs of your army skilled in warfare, of proven valor, brave, and honored and respected by you?

**2.5.38**  Do you give the food and wages of the army, which are due at the proper time, as fitting, without delay?

**2.5.39**  For when food and wages are overdue, retainers become angry with their master on account of their hardship; that is said to be a very great misfortune.

**2.5.40**  Are all the noble sons of good families principally devoted to you? Do they constantly sacrifice their lives for your cause in battle?

**2.5.41**  Does not one person, overcome by desire, transgressing your authority, govern all the many affairs leading to the next world just as he pleases?

**2.5.42**  Does a man who adorns his work through his personal effort receive greater honor or additional food and wages?

**2.5.43**  Do you honor with gifts, according to their merit and their virtues, men who are trained in learning and skilled in knowledge?

**2.5.44**  O bull of the Bharatas, do you support the wives of men who have met death in your cause and those who have fallen into misfortune?

**2.5.45**  O Partha, do you protect like a son an enemy who has submitted out of fear, or who has come unarmed, or who has been defeated in battle?

**2.5.46**  O lord of the earth, are you equitable toward all the earth and beyond suspicion, like a mother and like a father?

**2.5.47**  O bull of the Bharatas, when you hear that an enemy is in difficulty, do you attack him swiftly after examining the three kinds of strength?

**2.5.48**  O great king, having ascertained the rear guard, the determination of the enemy, and the strength of the army, and having paid wages in advance —

**2.5.49**  O scorcher of enemies, do you present to the chiefs of the enemy's army, in secret within their kingdom, gifts of jewels as each deserves?

**2.5.50**  O Partha, having first conquered yourself and mastered your senses, do you seek to conquer your enemies — those who are negligent and have not mastered their senses?

**2.5.51**  Do the well-practised virtues — conciliation, gifts, sowing dissension, and chastisement — go before you when you march against enemies?

**2.5.52**  O lord of the people, do you set out on campaign only after securing your base, strive to conquer your foes, and then protect what you have won?

**2.5.53**  Is your army, composed of the fourfold force and endowed with its eight constituents, well led by its generals so as to repel your enemies?

**2.5.54**  O scorcher of foes, O great king, do you in battle destroy your enemies without leaving behind even the smallest gleaning or handful in a foreign kingdom?

**2.5.55**  Are many officials appointed by you in both your own and enemy kingdoms, who look after your interests and protect one another?

**2.5.56**  O great king, do those whom you have authorized guard against dangers from things that are eaten, that touch the body, and that are smelled?

**2.5.57**  Are your treasury, granary, conveyances, gates, armoury, and revenues maintained by your loyal servants who have been consecrated with auspicious rites?

**2.5.58**  O lord of the people, do you first protect yourself from both internal and external dangers, and then protect your subjects from one another?

**2.5.59**  Do they not publicly declare, in the morning hours, your expenditure incurred through vices such as drinking, gambling, gaming, and women?

**2.5.60**  Is your expenditure settled with half of your income, or with a quarter, or with three-quarters of the quarter-share?

**2.5.61**  Do you repeatedly show favour with wealth and grain to your kinsmen, elders, the aged, merchants, artisans, and those who have taken refuge with you, especially those in distress?

**2.5.62**  Are all your accountants and scribes employed in income and expenditure attending daily in the morning to your revenues and disbursements?

**2.5.63**  Do you refrain from removing from their offices those who are experienced in affairs, who desire your welfare, and who speak what is pleasing, without first finding any fault in them?

**2.5.64**  O Bharata, having recognized men as superior, inferior, or middling, do you assign them to tasks befitting each?

**2.5.65**  O lord of the people, are the greedy, the dishonest, the hostile, or those who have not yet attained experience in affairs excluded from your service?

**2.5.66**  Is your kingdom not oppressed by the greedy, by thieves, by princes, by a feminine faction, or by yourself? Are the farmers prosperous?

**2.5.67**  In your kingdom, are there large tanks, full of water, distributed in every region, so that agriculture does not depend solely on rain?

**2.5.68**  Do you ensure that the farmer does not lack for seed or food, and do you lend money as a favour at a hundred for one?

**2.5.69**  O dear one, is agriculture and trade properly maintained by honest men in your kingdom? For, O dear one, by trade and agriculture this world prospers happily.

**2.5.70**  O king, are groups of five wise men, duly purified and installed, jointly ensuring the security of your realm?

**2.5.71**  For the protection of your capital, are villages made as strong as cities, is the defence arranged as in a village, and are all these villages devoted to that purpose?

**2.5.72**  Do former robbers, accompanied by troops and under proper supervision, patrol both the accessible and inaccessible parts of your territory?

**2.5.73**  Do you treat women with kindness? Are they well protected? Do you not trust them blindly? Do you not disclose secrets to them?

**2.5.74**  Having heard your spies at night, having deliberated upon what must be done, and having learned the state of those within your household, do you sleep enjoying pleasant things?

**2.5.75**  O lord of the people, do you sleep during the first two watches of the night and then, rising in the last watch, contemplate dharma and artha?

**2.5.76**  O knower of the right moment, O son of Pandu, do you daily show yourself adorned with ornaments to the people, rising at the proper hour together with your ministers?

**2.5.77**  O subduer of enemies, do guards wearing red garments, with swords in hand and fully adorned, attend upon you on all sides for your protection?

**2.5.78**  O lord of the people, do you, after proper examination, act like Yama toward those who deserve punishment and fittingly toward those who deserve honour, whether they be disagreeable or agreeable to you?

**2.5.79**  O son of Prtha, do you always ward off bodily ailments with medicines and discipline, and mental ailments by attending on the aged?

**2.5.80**  Are physicians skilled in the eightfold system of medicine, who are friends and devoted to you, always present for the benefit of your body?

**2.5.81**  O lord of the people, do you never dismiss out of pride, delusion, or desire the plaintiffs and defendants who have come before you?

**2.5.82**  Do you refrain from blocking the livelihood of those who have sought your protection out of greed, delusion, misplaced confidence, or affection?

**2.5.83**  Are the citizens and the inhabitants of your realm not in any way jointly alienated from you, having been bought over by enemies?

**2.5.84**  O Yudhishthira, is a weak enemy who is being pressed hard by a stronger power brought over by counsel, or a strong one by force, or each by means of both?

**2.5.85**  Are all the foremost rulers of the earth devoted to you, and do those whom you have won over surrender their very lives in your cause?

**2.5.86**  Is honour paid by you, according to their merit, in all branches of learning to brahmins and virtuous persons for your own supreme good and auspiciousness?

**2.5.87**  Do you abide in the dharma rooted in the Veda, as it was practised by people of old, and do you strive to perform that duty likewise?

**2.5.88**  Do brahmins eat tasty, wholesome, and meritorious food in your house under your oversight, along with proper gifts?

**2.5.89**  With a single-pointed mind and self-mastery, do you strive to perform sacrifices, Vajapeya rites in their entirety, and Pundarika rites completely?

**2.5.90**  Do you bow in reverence to kinsmen, teachers, the aged, the gods, ascetics, sacred trees of good omen, and brahmins?

**2.5.91**  O sinless one, is this understanding yours and this your conduct — one that promotes long life and fame and is guided by dharma, desire, and material welfare?

**2.5.92**  For a king who lives by this understanding, his kingdom does not sink; having conquered the earth, he thrives in supreme happiness.

**2.5.93**  Is a noble man of pure soul, though accused of theft, not put to death out of greed by those who are unskilled in examining evidence through the legal texts?

**2.5.94**  O bull among men, is a thief who has been questioned, seized, proven guilty by experts with good cause, not released out of greed for his wealth?

**2.5.95**  O Bharata, when a case is being judged, do your ministers, bribed with money, not misrepresent the interests of both the rich and the poor?

**2.5.96**  Atheism, falsehood, anger, heedlessness, procrastination, not consulting the wise, sloth, and a restless mind —

**2.5.97**  — pondering affairs alone, or deliberating with those ignorant of what is harmful, failing to begin what has been decided, and not guarding one's counsels —

**2.5.98**  — not performing auspicious rites, and addiction to sensual pleasures — do you avoid these fourteen royal faults?

**2.5.99**  Are your Vedas fruitful? Is your wealth fruitful? Is your wife fruitful? Is your learning fruitful?

**2.5.100**  Yudhishthira said: How indeed are the Vedas fruitful? How indeed is wealth fruitful? How indeed is a wife fruitful? How indeed is learning fruitful?

**2.5.101**  Narada said: The Vedas bear fruit in the Agnihotra sacrifice; wealth bears fruit in what is given away and enjoyed; a wife bears fruit in pleasure and offspring; learning bears fruit in good character and conduct.

**2.5.102**  Vaishampayana said: Having declared all this, the sage Narada of surpassing austerity then asked this further question of the righteous-souled Yudhishthira.

**2.5.103**  Narada said: Are merchants who have come from afar for the sake of gain assessed the customs duty exactly as prescribed by those who live by collecting customs?

**2.5.104**  O king, are the men honoured by you in the city and in the countryside bringing their goods without being deceived by tricks?

**2.5.105**  O dear one, do you always listen to the words of the aged who are well versed in dharma and artha, those who know the meaning of things and those who perceive dharma?

**2.5.106**  For the sake of dharma and artha, is honey and clarified butter given to the twice-born from the produce of your agricultural estates, cattle herds, and gardens of flowers and fruits?

**2.5.107**  Do you regularly and properly provide tools and materials to all artisans at least every four months?

**2.5.108**  O great king, do you recognize work well done, do you praise the one who did it, and do you honour and worship him in the assembly of the good?

**2.5.109**  O bull of the Bharatas, O master, do you study all the manuals — the manuals on elephants, on horses, and on chariots?

**2.5.110**  O bull of the Bharatas, are the treatise on the science of archery and the treatise on siege engines and city fortification constantly practised in your household?

**2.5.111**  O sinless one, are all weapons, the Brahma staff, and all the uses of poison known to you for the destruction of enemies?

**2.5.112**  Do you protect your own kingdom from the danger of fire, from the danger of serpents and wild beasts, and from the disturbance caused by disease?

**2.5.113**  O knower of dharma, do you protect like a father the blind, the dumb, the lame, the maimed, the friendless, and also the wandering ascetics?

**2.5.114**  Vaishampayana said: The great-souled bull of the Kurus, having heard these words of the best of brahmins, bowed at his feet in salutation and, filled with joy, the king spoke to Narada of godlike form.

**2.5.115**  "I shall act exactly as you have told me; for my wisdom has thereby grown still further." Having spoken thus, the king acted accordingly and gained the earth girdled by the ocean.

**2.5.116**  Narada said: A king who conducts himself thus in the protection of the four orders, having enjoyed great happiness here, attains to the same world as Indra.

**2.6.1**  Vaishampayana said: Having duly worshipped him and been given leave by the word of the great sage, the dharma-king Yudhishthira then spoke in orderly reply.

**2.6.2**  O blessed one, you have spoken what is right and the proper determination of dharma. To the best of my ability and in accordance with what is proper, this rule is followed by me.

**2.6.3**  What was to be done by kings of old, precisely that — there is no doubt — was done in a manner conforming to what is proper, well-reasoned, and purposeful.

**2.6.4**  O lord, we desire to walk in that righteous path of theirs; but it is not possible to walk it in exactly the same way as those self-disciplined ones did.

**2.6.5**  Having spoken thus, the righteous-souled one honoured those words, and after a moment, seeing the sage who wanders through the worlds —

**2.6.6**  — Narada, seated at his ease — Yudhishthira, the son of Pandu, of great mind, sitting near him in the midst of the kings, asked him:

**2.6.7**  "You always move, swift as thought, through the many and various worlds created of old by Brahma, observing them all.

**2.6.8**  O brahmin, has any assembly hall like this one ever been seen by you anywhere, or one superior to this? Tell me, I am asking."

**2.6.9**  Having heard this utterance of the dharma-king, Narada replied to the son of Pandu with a smile, in a sweet voice:

**2.6.10**  "O dear one, O king, O Bharata, I have not previously seen nor heard of any hall made of gems among humans equal to yours.

**2.6.11**  But I shall describe the hall of Yama, king of the ancestors, and of the wise Varuna, and of Indra, and of him who dwells on Kailasa.

**2.6.12**  I shall also describe the divine hall of Brahma, free from all weariness — if your mind inclines to hear it, O bull of the Bharatas."

**2.6.13**  When thus addressed by Narada, the dharma-king Yudhishthira, with hands joined in reverence, surrounded by his brothers and by all those kings,

**2.6.14**  — the dharma-king of great mind — replied to Narada thus: "Describe all those assembly halls; we desire to hear of them."

**2.6.15**  Of what materials are those assembly halls, O brahmin? What is their extent and length? And who attends upon the Grandfather in that assembly?

**2.6.16**  Who attends upon Vāsava the king of gods, upon Yama the son of Vivasvat, upon Varuṇa, and upon Kubera in their assemblies?

**2.6.17**  All of this in its true detail, O divine seer — we wish to hear it from you as you speak; our curiosity is very great.

**2.6.18**  Thus addressed by the Pāṇḍava, Nārada replied to him: "In order, O king, let those divine halls be heard by us now."

**2.7.1**  Nārada said: The divine assembly of Śakra is radiant, won by his deeds; built by Śakra himself, O Kauravya, it blazes like the sun.

**2.7.2**  It is a hundred yojanas wide and one hundred and fifty yojanas long; it moves through the sky at will and rises five yojanas high.

**2.7.3**  Free from old age, sorrow, and fatigue, without fear, auspicious and fair, furnished with rooms and seats, delightful, adorned with divine trees.

**2.7.4**  In that assembly, O Pārtha, the lord of gods sits on the highest throne, together with Śacī the wife of Mahendra, with Śrī and Lakṣmī, O Bhārata.

**2.7.5**  Wearing a form beyond description, crowned, with red armlets, clad in spotless garments, adorned with varied garlands, accompanied by Modesty, Fame, and Splendour.

**2.7.6**  In that assembly, O king, the Maruts always attend the great-souled Śatakratu from all sides — all the householder-gods, the Siddhas, the divine seers, the Sādhyas, and the hosts of gods as well.

**2.7.7**  All of these, with their attendants, bearing divine forms and well-adorned, attend upon the great-souled king of gods, the subduer of enemies.

**2.7.8**  Likewise all the divine seers, O Pārtha, attend upon Śakra — spotless, their sins shaken off, blazing like fires, radiant, united with Soma, free from evil, weariness gone.

**2.7.9**  Parāśara and Parvata, and likewise Sāvarṇi and Gālava, Śaṅkha and Likhita, and the sage Gauraśiras as well.

**2.7.10**  Durvāsas the long-ascetic, and Yājñavalkya, Bhāluki, Uddālaka, Śvetaketu, and the mighty Śāṭyāyana.

**2.7.11**  Haviṣmān and Gaviṣṭha, and king Hariścandra, Hṛdya and Udaraśāṇḍilya, Pārāśarya and Kṛṣīvala.

**2.7.12**  Vātaskandha and Viśākha, Vidhātā and Kāla himself, Anantadanta and Tvaṣṭā, Viśvakarmā and Tumburu.

**2.7.13**  Those born not of the womb and those born of the womb, those who live on wind and those who feed on oblations — they all attend upon Īśāna, the lord of all worlds, the bearer of the thunderbolt.

**2.7.14**  Sahadeva and Sunītha, and Vālmīki of great austerity, Samīka and Satyavān, Pracetas and Satyasaṃgara.

**2.7.15**  Medhātithi and Vāmadeva, Pulastya and Pulaha, Kratu, Marutta and Marīci, Sthāṇu and Atri of great austerity.

**2.7.16**  Kakṣīvān and Gautama, Tārkṣya and the sage Vaiśvānara, the sage Kālakavṛkṣīya, Āśrāvya and Hiraṇyada, Saṃvarta and Devahavya, and the mighty Viṣvaksena.

**2.7.17**  The divine waters and the herbs, Faith and Intellect, Sarasvatī, Wealth and Dharma and Desire, and Lightning too, O Pāṇḍava.

**2.7.18**  The rain-bearing clouds, the winds, the thunders, the Eastern Direction, the sacrifice-bearers, and the twenty-seven sacred fires.

**2.7.19**  Agni and Soma, Indra and Agni together, Mitra and Savitṛ, Aryaman, Bhaga, the Viśve-devās, the Sādhyas, Śukra and Manthī, O Bhārata.

**2.7.20**  The sacrifices and the sacrificial fees, the planets, all the Stobha chants, and whatever mantras carry the sacrifice — all of them are assembled there.

**2.7.21**  So too the Apsarases, O king, and the charming Gandharvas, with dance and instrumental music and song, and with varied forms of laughter, they delight the king of gods, the Śatakratu.

**2.7.22**  Praising him with hymns and auspicious words, extolling him for his deeds and his exploits — the great-souled slayer of Bala and Vṛtra.

**2.7.23**  All the brahmin royal sages, and all the divine seers as well, in varied celestial aerial cars blazing like fires.

**2.7.24**  Garlanded and adorned, some go and others come; Bṛhaspati and Śukra both come to that assembly together.

**2.7.25**  These and many others, of controlled minds and disciplined vows, in aerial cars bright as the moon, lovely to behold as Soma — by Brahmā's word, O king, Bhṛgu and the Seven Sages as well.

**2.7.26**  This is the assembly, O king, crowned with lotus garlands, that I have seen — the assembly of Śatakratu, O great king. Now hear of the assembly of Yama, O sinless one.

**2.8.1**  Nārada said: I will describe the divine assembly hall, O Yudhiṣṭhira — understand it — the one that Viśvakarmā made for Vaivasvata's purpose.

**2.8.2**  That assembly of fire-like brilliance, O king, was a hundred yojanas in extent of breadth and length, and was very vast indeed, O Pāṇḍava.

**2.8.3**  Radiant as the sun, blazing with light, moving in all directions at will, neither too cold nor too hot, and filling the mind with delight.

**2.8.4**  In that hall there is no grief, no old age, no hunger or thirst, nothing unpleasant, no misery or fatigue, nor anything adverse whatsoever.

**2.8.5**  All desires, both divine and human, are present there, and food to eat and drink in abundance and full of flavor, O subduer of enemies.

**2.8.6**  There are garlands of sacred fragrance, trees bearing flowers and fruits perpetually, and waters full of taste, both cold and warm.

**2.8.7**  In that assembly, holy royal sages and pure brahmin sages attend with joy upon Yama the son of Vivasvat, O dear one.

**2.8.8**  Yayāti and Nahuṣa, Pūru, Māndhātā, Somaka, and Nṛga, Trasadasyu and Turaya, Kṛtavīrya and Śrutaśravas.

**2.8.9**  Aripraṇut and Susiṃha, Kṛtavega and Kṛti, Nimi, Pratardana, Śibi, Matsya, Pṛthvakṣa, and Bṛhadratha.

**2.8.10**  Aiḍa, Marutta, Kuśika, Sāṃkāśya, Sāṃkṛti, Bhava, Caturaśva, Sadaśvormi, and the king Kārtavīrya.

**2.8.11**  Bharata and Suratha, Sunītha, Naiṣadha Nala, Divodāsa and Sumanā, Ambarīṣa and Bhagīratha.

**2.8.12**  Vyaśva, Sadaśva, Vadhryaśva, Pañcahasta, Pṛthuśravas, Ruṣadgu, Vṛṣasena, and the very mighty Kṣupa.

**2.8.13**  Ruṣadaśva, Vasumanās, Purukutsa, the banner-bearer and chariot-warrior, Ārṣṭiṣeṇa, Dilīpa, and the great-souled Uśīnara.

**2.8.14**  Auśīnara, Puṇḍarīka, Śaryāti, Śarabha, Śuci, Aṅga, Ariṣṭa, Vena, Duḥṣanta, Saṃjaya, and Jaya.

**2.8.15**  Bhāṅgāsvari and Sunītha, Niṣadha and Tviṣīratha, Karaṃdhama and Bāhlika, Sudyumna, the mighty Madhu.

**2.8.16**  Kapotaromā, Tṛṇaka, Sahadeva and Arjuna as well, Rāma the son of Daśaratha, and Lakṣmaṇa, and Pratardana.

**2.8.17**  Alarka and Kakṣasena, Gaya and Gaurāśva, Rāma the son of Jamadagni as well, Nābhāga and Sagara.

**2.8.18**  Bhūridyumna and Mahāśva, Pṛtvaśva and Janaka, king Vainya, Vāriṣeṇa, Puruja, and Janamejaya.

**2.8.19**  Brahmadatta and Trigarta, the sky-roaming king Uparicara, Indradyumna, Bhīmajānu, Gaya, Pṛṣṭha, the sinless Naya.

**2.8.20**  Padma and Mucukunda, Bhūridyumna, Prasenajit, Ariṣṭanemi, Pradyumna, Pṛthagaśva, and Aja.

**2.8.21**  A hundred Matsya kings, a hundred Nīpas, a hundred Hayas, one hundred and one Dhṛtarāṣṭras, eighty Janamejayās.

**2.8.22**  A hundred Brahmadattas, a hundred of the Īriṇas and Vairiṇas, and the royal sage Śaṃtanu, and Pāṇḍu your own father.

**2.8.23**  Uśadgava, Śataratha, the king of gods Jayadratha, and the royal sage Vṛṣādarbhi together with his minister Dhāman.

**2.8.24**  And others in thousands — those Śaśabindus who have gone there, having performed many great horse-sacrifices with abundant fees.

**2.8.25**  These holy royal sages, famous and widely learned, attend upon Vaivasvata in that assembly, O royal sage.

**2.8.26**  Agastya and Mataṅga, and likewise Kāla and Mṛtyu, the sacrificers and the Siddhas, and those whose bodies are made of yoga.

**2.8.27**  The Agniṣvātta ancestors and those who drink foam and those who drink steam, those who partake of the ritual offering, those who sit on the sacred grass, and others who have taken bodily form.

**2.8.28**  The wheel of Time itself in visible form, and the blessed fire the carrier of oblations, and men of evil deeds who die during the southern course of the sun.

**2.8.29**  Those who are yoked to Kāla's leading, and the attendants of Yama — in that assembly the Śiṃśapa and Pālāśa trees, as well as Kāśa and Kuśa grasses and others, attend in embodied form upon the king of dharma, free from affliction.

**2.8.30**  These and many others are the members of the assembly of the king of ancestors — it is impossible to enumerate them all by name and deeds.

**2.8.31**  That hall is unobstructed, O Pārtha, delightful, moving at will — built by Viśvakarmā after performing austerities for a long time.

**2.8.32**  Blazing with its own splendour as if on fire, O Bhārata — to that hall go those of fierce austerity, of excellent vows, speakers of truth.

**2.8.33**  Peaceful, renunciant, accomplished, purified by holy deeds — all of them with radiant bodies and all clad in spotless garments.

**2.8.34**  With varied armlets and varied garlands, all with blazing earrings, adorned with the trappings of holy and meritorious deeds.

**2.8.35**  Great-souled Gandharvas in their hundreds and hosts of Apsarases — with instruments, dance and song, laughter and graceful movement in full measure.

**2.8.36**  Sacred fragrances and sounds pervade that hall on all sides, O Pārtha, and divine garlands serve it from every quarter.

**2.8.37**  A hundred times a hundred thousand righteous beings, endowed with beauty and intelligence, attend upon that great-souled lord of creatures.

**2.8.38**  Such is that assembly hall, O king, of the great-souled king of the ancestors. I will also describe the lotus-garlanded assembly of Varuṇa.

**2.9.1**  Nārada said: O Yudhiṣṭhira, the divine assembly of Varuṇa is white-gleaming; in size it matches the hall of Yama, with auspicious walls and gateways.

**2.9.2**  Built within the waters by Viśvakarmā, it is adorned with divine jewel-made trees that bestow fruits and flowers.

**2.9.3**  With trees of blue, yellow, white, dark, and red hues as well, with drooping boughs and with shrubs bearing clusters of blossoms.

**2.9.4**  And in that hall are birds of many forms with sweet voices, of indescribable beauty, by the hundreds and by the thousands.

**2.9.5**  That assembly hall has a pleasant touch, neither cold nor hot; furnished with rooms and seats, delightful, white, and guarded by Varuṇa.

**2.9.6**  In it sits Varuṇa together with Vāruṇī, O Bhārata, wearing a robe of divine gems, adorned and shining with ornaments.

**2.9.7**  Garlanded and adorned, trailing divine garlands, the Ādityas attend there upon Varuṇa the lord of waters.

**2.9.8**  Vāsuki and Takṣaka, and the nāga Airāvata as well, Kṛṣṇa and Lohita, and the mighty Padma and Citra.

**2.9.9**  The two nāgas Kambala and Aśvatara, Dhṛtarāṣṭra and Balāhaka, Maṇimān, Kuṇḍaladhara, Karkoṭaka and Dhanaṃjaya.

**2.9.10**  Prahlāda and Mūṣikāda, and likewise Janamejaya — all of them bearing banners and rings on their hoods, all possessed of hoods.

**2.9.11**  These and many other serpents, O Yudhiṣṭhira, attend in that hall upon the great-souled Varuṇa, free from weariness.

**2.9.12**  Bali the son of Virocana, king Naraka, Pṛthivīṃjaya, Prahlāda and Vipracitti, and all the Kālakhañjas.

**2.9.13**  Suhanu, Durmukha, Śaṅkha, Sumanā, Sumati, Svana, Ghaṭodara, Mahāpārśva, Krathana, and likewise Piṭhara —

**2.9.14**  Viśvarūpa, Surūpa, Virūpa, Mahāśiras, Daśagrīva, Vālī, Meghavāsā, and Daśāvara —

**2.9.15**  Kaiṭabha, Viṭaṭūta, Saṃhrāda, and Indratāpana — all these hosts of Daityas and Dānavas, wearing beautiful earrings,

**2.9.16**  all garlanded, all wearing crowns, all adorned with divine ornaments, all having obtained boons, all heroes, all free from death —

**2.9.17**  they, stationed by the noose of righteousness, ever attend upon the god Varuṇa in that hall, all great-souled and firm in virtuous vows.

**2.9.18**  Likewise the four oceans, the river Bhāgīrathī, Kālindī, Vidiśā, Veṇṇā, and the swift-flowing Narmadā —

**2.9.19**  Vipāśā, Śatadrū, Candrabhāgā, Sarasvatī, Irāvatī, Vitastā, the Sindhu, and the divine river Devanadat —

**2.9.20**  Godāvarī, Kṛṣṇaveṇṇā, the noble river Kāverī — these and other rivers, as well as holy fords and lakes,

**2.9.21**  wells with springs, O Yudhiṣṭhira, embodied pools and tanks, O Bhārata — all having taken bodily form —

**2.9.22**  the directions, the earth itself, all the mountains, and all aquatic creatures — they attend upon that great-souled one.

**2.9.23**  Troops of Gandharvas and Apsarases, equipped with song and music, praising Varuṇa — all of them assemble in that hall.

**2.9.24**  The mountains bearing precious gems, the subterranean waters in which minerals rest — all of them, having taken form, attend upon that lord.

**2.9.25**  This lovely hall of Varuṇa was seen by me in passing, O bull of the Bharatas. Now hear of the hall of Kubera.

**2.10.1**  Nārada said: The hall of Vaiśravaṇa, O king, is a hundred yojanas long, seventy yojanas wide, and gleaming white.

**2.10.2**  It was fashioned by Vaiśravaṇa himself through asceticism, O king; it has the radiance of the moon, floats in the sky, and resembles the peak of Kailāsa.

**2.10.3**  Borne by Guhyakas, it appears as if suspended in the sky; it is divine and adorned by tall golden trees.

**2.10.4**  Radiant and luminous, filled with divine fragrance, delightful, shaped like the peak of a white cloud — it appears to float.

**2.10.5**  In that hall, King Vaiśravaṇa sits resplendent, wearing ornaments and garments of many kinds, surrounded by thousands of women, his earrings blazing with light.

**2.10.6**  He is seated on a supreme throne, holy as the sun itself, spread with divine coverings and furnished with divine footrests.

**2.10.7**  The pure wind, carrying fragrances from the splendid groves of Mandāra trees and from the Saugandhika forests —

**2.10.8**  and from the lotus-ponds of the city named Alakā, and from the sandalwood forests — that breeze, delighting the heart, wafts about him.

**2.10.9**  There, O Bhārata, gods together with Gandharvas, surrounded by troops of Apsarases, sing divine songs with divine melodic grace.

**2.10.10**  Miśrakeśī, Rambhā, Citrasena, Śucismitā, Cārunetrā, Ghṛtācī, Menakā, Puñjikasthalā —

**2.10.11**  Viśvācī, Sahajanyā, Pramlocā, Urvaśī, Irā, Vargā, Saurabheyī, Samīcī, Budbudā, and Latā —

**2.10.12**  these and thousands of other Apsarases, skilled in dance and song, attend upon Dhanadhada, O Pāṇḍava.

**2.10.13**  Unceasingly, with divine music, dance, and song, that hall shines lovely and ever full, thronged with bands of Gandharvas and Apsarases.

**2.10.14**  Among the Gandharvas are the Kiṃnaras by name; others are called Naras. There too are Maṇibhadra, Dhanada, the Guhyaka Śvetabhadra —

**2.10.15**  Kaśeraka, Gaṇḍakaṇḍu, the mighty Pradyota, Kustumburu, the Piśāca, Gajakarṇa, and Viśālaka —

**2.10.16**  Varāhakarṇa, Sāndroṣṭha, Phalabhakṣa, Phalodaka, Aṅgacūḍa, Śikhāvarta, Hemanetra, and Vibhīṣaṇa —

**2.10.17**  Puṣpānana, Piṅgalaka, Śoṇitoda, Pravālaka, one whose home is in the treetops, and Cīravāsā — O Bhārata —

**2.10.18**  these and many other Yakṣas, hundreds of thousands of them, and the blessed goddess Śrī herself, and likewise Nalakūbara —

**2.10.19**  and I myself have visited it often, and others like me; teachers were present there, and divine sages as well.

**2.10.20**  The blessed lord — Umā's husband, Paśupati, bearer of the trident, destroyer of Bhaga's eyes —

**2.10.21**  three-eyed Tryambaka, O tiger among kings, and the Devī free from fatigue — surrounded by dwarfs, hideous beings, hunchbacks, red-eyed ones, and beings swift as the mind —

**2.10.22**  by those who eat flesh, fat, and marrow, terrible to hear and to see, bearing various dreadful weapons, swift as the wind — thus attended, he ever sits with his friend Dhanada, O king.

**2.10.23**  Such was the hall I saw floating in the sky, O king. Now, O king, I shall describe the hall of the Grandsire, free from all fatigue.

**2.11.1**  Nārada said: Long ago, O king, in the age of the gods, the blessed Āditya descended from heaven to the world of men, free from fatigue, wishing to see it.

**2.11.2**  Roaming in human form, having seen the hall of Svayambhū, he described to me truly that Brāhmic hall, O Pāṇḍava.

**2.11.3**  That hall is of immeasurable splendor, divine, born of the mind, O bull of the Bharatas, indescribable in its power, and charming to all beings.

**2.11.4**  Hearing of the qualities of that hall, O son of Pāṇḍu, I desired to see it, and so, O king, I addressed Āditya:

**2.11.5**  "Blessed one, I wish to see the hall of the Grandsire. By what asceticism, O lord of cattle, or by what deed is it possible to see it?

**2.11.6**  Or by what efficacious herbs, or by what magical power? Tell me, O blessed one — how might I see that hall?"

**2.11.7**  Then that mighty, blessed Sūrya, taking me with him, went to that Brāhmic hall, free from sin and from all fatigue.

**2.11.8**  "It is of such-and-such a form" — even so it cannot be described, O lord of men, for from moment to moment it assumes another form that is equally beyond description.

**2.11.9**  I know not its dimensions, nor its configuration, O Bhārata; nor have I ever before seen a form like it.

**2.11.10**  That hall is supremely pleasant, O king — neither cold nor hot; those who enter it experience no hunger, no thirst, and no weariness.

**2.11.11**  It seems fashioned of many forms, wonderfully varied and brilliantly luminous; it is not supported by pillars; it is eternal and does not decay.

**2.11.12**  Shining by its own light, it blazes beyond the moon, the sun, and fire; stationed in the vault of heaven, it seems to outshine even the sun.

**2.11.13**  In that hall, the blessed Grandsire of the worlds sits alone, unceasingly governing the worlds through divine power.

**2.11.14**  The lords of creatures also attend upon him as their master: Dakṣa, Pracetas, Pulaha, Marīci, and likewise Kaśyapa —

**2.11.15**  Bhṛgu, Atri, Vasiṣṭha, Gautama, and Aṅgiras; Mind, Space, the Sciences, Wind, Fire, Water, and Earth —

**2.11.16**  Sound, Touch, Form, Taste, and Smell, O Bhārata; Primordial Nature, its modifications, and whatever other causes exist in the world —

**2.11.17**  the Moon together with the lunar mansions, the radiant Āditya, the winds, the sacred rites, Resolve, and the vital breath —

**2.11.18**  these and many others are present before Svayambhū; Artha, Dharma, and Kāma; Joy, Aversion, Austerity, and Self-restraint —

**2.11.19**  Gandharvas and Apsarases come there together, twenty-seven of the other kinds, and all the guardians of the worlds in every direction.

**2.11.20**  Śukra, Bṛhaspati, Budha, Aṅgāraka, Śanaiścara, Rāhu, and all the planets likewise —

**2.11.21**  Mantra, Rathaṃtara, Harimān, Vasumān, the Ādityas with their king — designated by the various pairs of names —

**2.11.22**  the Maruts, Viśvakarman, the Vasus, O Bhārata, all the hosts of Pitṛs, and all the oblations —

**2.11.23**  the Ṛgveda, the Sāmaveda, the Yajurveda, O Pāṇḍava, and likewise the Atharvaveda, and the sacred junctures of time, O lord of the people —

**2.11.24**  the Itihāsas, the Upavedas, and all the Vedāṅgas, the planets, the sacrifices, Soma, and all the deities —

**2.11.25**  the Sāvitrī, the Durgataraṇī, the sevenfold Vāṇī, Intellect, Steadiness, Learning, Wisdom, Understanding, Renown, and Patience —

**2.11.26**  Sāman hymns, Stuti verses, Śastra recitations, various Gāthā songs, and logical commentaries — all in embodied form, O lord of the people —

**2.11.27**  Kṣaṇas, Lavas, Muhūrtas, day and night likewise, the fortnights, months, and the six seasons, O Bhārata —

**2.11.28**  the years, the fivefold Yuga cycle, the four kinds of day-and-night reckonings, and the divine Wheel of Time, eternal, inexhaustible, and unchanging —

**2.11.29**  Aditi, Diti, Danu, Surasā, Vinatā, Irā, Kālakā, the goddess Surabhi, Saramā, and Gautamī —

**2.11.30**  the Ādityas, the Vasus, the Rudras, the Maruts, the two Aśvins, the Viśvedevas, the Sādhyas, and the swift-minded Pitṛs —

**2.11.31**  Rākṣasas, Piśācas, Dānavas, Guhyakas, Suparṇas, Nāgas, and animals — all attend upon the Grandsire.

**2.11.32**  The god Nārāyaṇa is there, as well as the divine sages, the Vālakhilya sages, and both the womb-born and those not born of wombs.

**2.11.33**  Whatever is seen in these three worlds, moving or stationary — all of it I saw in that hall. Know this, O lord of men.

**2.11.34**  Eighty-eight thousand celibate ascetics, O Pāṇḍava, and fifty of the sages who have offspring —

**2.11.35**  all those celestial beings, having seen him as they wished, bow their heads to him and return by the way they came.

**2.11.36**  Gods, Daityas, Nāgas, sages, Yakṣas, Suparṇas, Kāleyas, Gandharvas, and Apsarases who arrive as guests —

**2.11.37**  these greatly fortunate ones — Brahmā, the Grandsire of the worlds, of boundless intelligence and compassionate toward all beings, receives fittingly.

**2.11.38**  And having received them, the Soul of the Universe, Svayambhū of immeasurable splendor, engages them with gentle words, honors, and pleasures, O lord of men.

**2.11.39**  And so, O Bhārata, with those coming and going, that hall is ever bustling, dear friend, and bestows happiness.

**2.11.40**  All-luminous, divine, attended by hosts of Brahmarṣis, blazing with Brāhmic glory, free from all fatigue — it shone magnificently.

**2.11.41**  Such was that hall I saw — unequalled in all the worlds. Among humans, O tiger among kings, it is like your own hall.

**2.11.42**  These halls among the gods I have seen before, O Pāṇḍava; yet your hall is the most excellent of all in the world of humans.

**2.11.43**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: You have described for the most part the company of kings, O best of speakers; and as for the hall of Vaivasvata, as you tell it, O lord —

**2.11.44**  in the hall of Varuṇa, O mighty one, you spoke of the Nāgas, the great Daitya lords, rivers, and oceans —

**2.11.45**  Likewise the yakṣas and guhyakas of Kubera, the lord of wealth, and the rākṣasas, the gandharvas and apsarases, and the blessed one whose banner bears the bull—all these are present there.

**2.11.46**  In the assembly hall of Pitāmaha, O sage, the great sages were described to you, as were all the hosts of the gods and all the śāstras as well.

**2.11.47**  In the assembly of Śatakratu, O sage, the gods were enumerated, and in summary the gandharvas of various kinds and the great sages.

**2.11.48**  But only one royal sage, the great-souled Hariścandra, O great sage, was described to you as a permanent resident of the assembly of the great Indra, lord of gods.

**2.11.49**  What deed did he perform, or what austerity and firm vow, by which he, the greatly renowned one, could rival even Śakra himself?

**2.11.50**  And, O brahmin, my greatly fortunate father Pāṇḍu, who has gone to the realm of the ancestors, was also seen by you — how did you come to meet him?

**2.11.51**  And what did he say, O blessed one? I wish to know this. I have the greatest curiosity to hear everything from you.

**2.11.52**  Nārada said: O king of kings, O lord, since you ask me about Hariścandra, I shall fully declare to you the greatness of that wise one.

**2.11.53**  That king was mighty, an emperor over all rulers of the earth; all the guardians of the earth stood bowing in obedience to his commands.

**2.11.54**  Mounting a single victorious chariot adorned with gold, he conquered by the might of his weapons all seven continents, O lord of men.

**2.11.55**  Having conquered the entire earth with its mountains, forests, and woodlands, O great king, he performed the Rājasūya, that great sacrifice.

**2.11.56**  At his command all the guardians of the earth brought their wealth, and they served as attendants to the twice-born at that sacrifice.

**2.11.57**  With joy the lord of men gave wealth to the officiating priests, giving five times more than what they had prescribed.

**2.11.58**  He also satisfied the brāhmaṇas, who had come from all directions, with varied goods and riches when the time of distribution arrived.

**2.11.59**  With various foods and eatables offered according to desire, and with floods of gems by which the twice-born were satisfied and gladdened — thus it was declared — he became radiant and glorious, superior to all other kings.

**2.11.60**  For this reason, O Pārtha, Hariścandra shines above those thousands of kings — know this, O bull of the Bharatas.

**2.11.61**  And Hariścandra the splendid, having completed the great sacrifice, was consecrated and shone with imperial sovereignty, O lord of men.

**2.11.62**  And those other guardians of the earth who have performed the Rājasūya, that great sacrifice, rejoice together with the great Indra, O Bhārata.

**2.11.63**  And those who have met their death on battlefields without fleeing — they, having reached that assembly, rejoice, O bull of the Bharatas.

**2.11.64**  And those who abandon their bodies here through intense austerity — they too, having attained that place, shine in splendor eternally.

**2.11.65**  And your father, Pāṇḍu, O son of Kuntī, O delight of the Kauravas, having seen the glory of the royal sage Hariścandra, was filled with wonder and said to you:

**2.11.66**  "You are capable of conquering the earth, your brothers stand under your command — perform the Rājasūya, the foremost of sacrifices, O Bhārata."

**2.11.67**  Fulfill his wish, O tiger among men, O son of Pāṇḍu — you shall go to share the same world as the great Indra together with your ancestors.

**2.11.68**  This great sacrifice is remembered as full of many obstacles, O king; for the brahma-rākṣasas who destroy sacrifices seek loopholes in it.

**2.11.69**  Battle, retreat, and the destruction of the earth — some occasion for ruin arises here as well.

**2.11.70**  Having considered all this, O king of kings, do what is proper. Be ever vigilant and active in protecting the four social orders; prosper, rejoice, and satisfy the twice-born with gifts.

**2.11.71**  This has been told to you in full, all that you asked of me. I take leave of you; I shall go to the city of the Dāśārhas.

**2.11.72**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having thus narrated to the sons of Pṛthā, O Janamejaya, O king, Nārada departed, accompanied by those same sages with whom he had arrived.

**2.11.73**  When Nārada had departed, the son of Pṛthā, together with his brothers, O Kaurava, began to contemplate the Rājasūya, the foremost of sacrifices, O Bhārata.

**2.12.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard the sage's words, Yudhiṣṭhira sighed deeply; pondering the attainment of the Rājasūya, he found no peace, O Bhārata.

**2.12.2**  For having heard that greatness of the great-souled royal sages, and having looked upon the attainment of the celestial world by sacrificers through their meritorious deeds,

**2.12.3**  and seeing above all the royal sage Hariścandra shining brilliantly, the sacrificer — he desired to perform the sacrifice, the Rājasūya.

**2.12.4**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, having honored all the members of the assembly, and having been honored by all of them in return, set his mind firmly upon the sacrifice.

**2.12.5**  He, the bull of the Kurus, O king of kings, turned his mind, thinking it over again and again, toward performing the Rājasūya sacrifice.

**2.12.6**  And he who possessed marvelous strength and vigor, ever upholding dharma, set his mind upon what would be beneficial for all the worlds.

**2.12.7**  Extending favor to all his subjects without distinction, Yudhiṣṭhira, the foremost among all who know dharma, acted for the welfare of all.

**2.12.8**  Things being thus, people took confidence in him as if he were their father; he had no enemy — hence his epithet Ajātaśatru, "he who has no born enemy."

**2.12.9**  He, the foremost among speakers, summoned his ministers and his brothers and consulted them again and again about the Rājasūya.

**2.12.10**  The ministers, thus questioned together, then spoke this purposeful reply to Yudhiṣṭhira, the greatly wise one who desired to sacrifice:

**2.12.11**  "The king who is consecrated with this rite attains the quality of Varuṇa; even being already a king, he seeks to attain the full quality of an emperor.

**2.12.12**  Your friends consider the time for the Rājasūya to have come for you, O delight of the Kurus, you who are worthy of the qualities of an emperor.

**2.12.13**  The occasion for that sacrifice is under your own control through the wealth of the kṣatriya; with the Sāman hymn, the six fires are built up therein by those of firm vows.

**2.12.14**  He who, taking up the oblations with the ladle, completes all the rites and undergoes the consecration at the sacrifice's end — he is called the conqueror of all.

**2.12.15**  You are capable, O mighty-armed one; we all stand under your command — without hesitation, O great king, set your mind upon the Rājasūya."

**2.12.16**  Thus all friends spoke, both separately and together; the son of Pāṇḍu, O lord of the people, hearing their words — so righteous, bold, dear, and most excellent — embraced them with his mind, he the slayer of foes.

**2.12.17**  Having heard those friends' words and knowing also what was fitting for himself, he set his mind upon the Rājasūya again and again, O Bhārata.

**2.12.18**  The wise one then again deliberated with his brothers, with the great-souled officiating priests, and with ministers headed by Dhaumya and Dvaipāyana.

**2.12.19**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: This desire that I have for the Rājasūya — of one worthy of imperial rank, of pure intention and faith — how might it come to be fulfilled?

**2.12.20**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus addressed by that king of lotus-like eyes, they spoke in due time these words to the righteous-souled Yudhiṣṭhira: "You are worthy, O knower of dharma, to perform the Rājasūya, that great sacrifice."

**2.12.21**  Then, when the king had been thus addressed by the officiating priests and sages, his ministers and brothers also honored those words with their approval.

**2.12.22**  But that greatly wise king, master of himself, the son of Pṛthā, reflected again and again for the sake of what would be beneficial to the worlds.

**2.12.23**  By examining the conjunction of capabilities, by properly weighing place and time, expenditure and income with discernment — the wise one who acts thus does not falter.

**2.12.24**  Understanding that the undertaking of a sacrifice does not lead merely to one's own ruin, he carried out what needed to be done with all diligence.

**2.12.25**  For the sake of resolving what must be done, he went in his mind to Kṛṣṇa Janārdana alone, considering Hari to be supreme above all the worlds.

**2.12.26**  The son of Pāṇḍu reflected upon the immeasurable, mighty-armed one — the unborn who took birth among men by his own will — judging him by deeds equal to those of the gods.

**2.12.27**  "Nothing is unknown to him, nothing is born not of his doing; there is nothing he cannot endure" — thus he thought of Kṛṣṇa.

**2.12.28**  Having thus made that firm resolution, Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Pṛthā straightway sent a messenger to the guru of all beings, honoring him as his own teacher.

**2.12.29**  That messenger, traveling swiftly with a fast chariot, reached the Yādavas and arrived at Dvārakā where Kṛṣṇa, dweller of Dvārakā, resided.

**2.12.30**  Acyuta, who longed to see the son of Pṛthā who longed to see him, then went with Indrasena to Indraprastha.

**2.12.31**  Passing through various lands with haste, his horses swift, Janārdana came to the son of Pṛthā who had gone to Indraprastha.

**2.12.32**  He was honored in the household by Dharmarāja as if by a brother, and then joyfully he saw also Bhīma and his father's sister's son.

**2.12.33**  Delighted, he then rejoiced together with his dear friend Arjuna and the twin sons of Yamā, who attended upon him as upon a teacher.

**2.12.34**  When Acyuta had rested in that pleasant place and had a moment's leisure in the morning, Dharmarāja approached him and made known his own purpose.

**2.12.35**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: The Rājasūya is desired by me, but it is not to be attained by mere desire alone; the means by which it is attained — that you know in full, O Kṛṣṇa.

**2.12.36**  He in whom all qualities are present, who is honored everywhere, and who is the lord over all kings — such a king attains the Rājasūya.

**2.12.37**  My friends, having assembled, say that Rājasūya must be performed by me; let my most certain resolution in this be made by your words, O Kṛṣṇa.

**2.12.38**  For some, out of friendship alone, do not point out faults; others likewise, for the sake of their own interests, speak only what is pleasing.

**2.12.39**  Some seek only what is pleasing, according to what they consider beneficial for themselves — and such, for the most part, is how people's counsel is seen in matters of purpose.

**2.12.40**  But you, transcending these motives and rising above desire and anger, ought to speak to us what is truly most fitting in the world.

**2.13.1**  Śrī Kṛṣṇa said: With all your qualities, O great king, you are worthy of the Rājasūya. Yet, knowing all this, I shall say something to you, O Bhārata.

**2.13.2**  The kṣatriya class that Rāma son of Jamadagni left remaining — from that descended what is now known in the world as the kṣatriya order.

**2.13.3**  This resolve of the lineage was made by the kṣatriyas, O lord of the earth, with words of command — that you know well, O bull of the Bharatas.

**2.13.4**  The nature of the lineage of Aila and of the Ikṣvāku dynasty is described: kings standing in ranks, and from them the other kṣatriyas on earth.

**2.13.5**  Those kings descended from the Aila lineage and likewise those of the Ikṣvāku line — know those to be one hundred and one clans, O bull of the Bharatas.

**2.13.6**  From Yayāti itself the great and greatly virtuous expansion of the Bhojas arose, and that expansion, O great king, extends in all four directions.

**2.13.7**  All the kṣatriyas attend upon that prosperity of theirs likewise; he, having enjoyed the central earth, thought of it in terms of mutual division.

**2.13.8**  But Jarāsaṃdha is another king at whose yoke a hundred kings have become subservient; he has attained imperial sovereignty by his origin.

**2.13.9**  Relying entirely upon that king, O greatly wise one, O king, the mighty Śiśupāla has indeed become a commander of his armies.

**2.13.10**  And waiting upon him alone as if a pupil, O great king, is Vakra, the lord of Karūṣa, a warrior of illusion and great strength.

**2.13.11**  And two others of great valor and great soul have taken refuge with the mighty Jarāsaṃdha — those two, Haṃsa and Ḍibhaka.

**2.13.12**  Dantavakra, Karūṣa, Kalabha, and Meghavāhana — bearing on their heads the divine jewel which is known as the bhūtamaṇi.

**2.13.13**  And he rules over Mura and Naraka, the two lords of the Yavanas — a king of boundless power in the west, like Varuṇa himself.

**2.13.14**  Bhagadatta, O great king, an old man and a friend of your father — he bows to Jarāsandha in word and in deed, O Bhārata.

**2.13.15**  Yet bound by affection, he is devoted to you in his heart like a father — and the king who guards the southern end of the west of the earth.

**2.13.16**  Your maternal uncle, the valiant Purujit Kuntivardhana — he alone submits to you out of affection, O scorcher of enemies.

**2.13.17**  Having thus gone over to Jarāsandha, there is one whom I did not slay before — the evil-minded one in the Cedi country who is known as the foremost of men.

**2.13.18**  He proclaims himself to be the foremost of men in this world and, deluded, constantly appropriates my own insignia as well.

**2.13.19**  Among the Vaṅgas, Puṇḍras, and Kirātas there is a king endowed with strength — that Pauṇḍraka who is renowned in the world as Vāsudeva.

**2.13.20**  He is endowed with four types of strength, O great king — a Bhoja, a friend of Indra, powerful, who by the strength of his knowledge conquered the Pāṇḍyas, Krathas, and Kaiśikas.

**2.13.21**  Whose brother Āhṛti is a hero equal to Jāmadagnya in battle — that king Bhīṣmaka, slayer of enemy heroes, is devoted to the Māgadha king.

**2.13.22**  He always acts agreeably toward his relations who bow before him — he who worships him does not honor us, being fixed in hostility toward us.

**2.13.23**  Not recognizing his own lineage or strength, O king, yet seeing his blazing fame, he has taken refuge with Jarāsandha.

**2.13.24**  And thus eighteen northern Bhoja clans, O lord, have sought refuge in the western direction out of fear of Jarāsandha.

**2.13.25**  The Śūrasenas, Bhadrakāras, Bodhas, Śālvas, Paṭaccaras, Sustharas, Sukuṭṭas, Kuṇindas together with the Kuntis —

**2.13.26**  And the kings of the Śālveyas with their brothers and followers, the southern Pāñcālas, those in the east among the Kuntis, the Kośalas —

**2.13.27**  And likewise, tormented by fear, abandoning the northern direction, the Matsyas and the Saṃnyastapādas have sought the southern direction.

**2.13.28**  Similarly all the Pāñcālas, tormented by fear of Jarāsandha, have abandoned their own kingdoms and fled in all directions.

**2.13.29**  Then after some time, Kaṃsa — crushing his kinsmen, the foolish one — came to the two daughters of the son of Bṛhadratha.

**2.13.30**  Those two were named Asti and Prāpti, younger sisters of Sahadeva — weak women. With that power, that fool overcame his kinsmen.

**2.13.31**  He had attained supremacy — that was an exceedingly great wrong — while the elders among the Bhoja kings and nobles were being oppressed by that evil-souled one.

**2.13.32**  By those who desired protection of their kinsmen, honor was shown to us — giving at that time the fair-bodied daughter of Āhuka's son to Akrūra.

**2.13.33**  The duty to my kinsmen was fulfilled by me, accompanied by Saṃkarṣaṇa — Kaṃsa and Sunāman were slain by me and by Rāma as well.

**2.13.34**  But when fear arose and Jarāsandha rose up in arms, this counsel was deliberated, O king, by the eighteen lesser clans together.

**2.13.35**  Even striking relentlessly with great weapons that kill by the hundreds, we could not destroy his army in three hundred years.

**2.13.36**  For in his army there were two foremost of warriors, equal to immortals in strength, the best among the mighty — known by the names Haṃsa and Ḍibhaka.

**2.13.37**  Those two heroes together and the valiant Jarāsandha — those three are sufficient for the three worlds, in my judgment.

**2.13.38**  Not only we, but all other kings as well — the wise among the wise held the same opinion about them.

**2.13.39**  Now there was a certain great king known as Haṃsa, and he along with others, O king, in battle with the eighteen lesser clans —

**2.13.40**  "Haṃsa has been slain" — so it was said by someone, O Bhārata. Hearing that, O king, Ḍibhaka submerged himself in the waters of the Yamunā.

**2.13.41**  "Without Haṃsa in this world I cannot bear to live" — with this resolve, Ḍibhaka met his end.

**2.13.42**  Having heard of Ḍibhaka's death, Haṃsa — the conqueror of enemy cities — himself also entered the Yamunā and drowned in it.

**2.13.43**  Hearing that those two had perished in the waters, king Jarāsandha departed to his own city of the Śūrasenas, O bull among Bharatas.

**2.13.44**  Then, O slayer of enemies, when that king had withdrawn, we all rejoiced and resided again in Mathurā.

**2.13.45**  But when that lotus-eyed wife of Kaṃsa, his daughter, approached her father, king Jarāsandha of Māgadha —

**2.13.46**  Grieving over her husband's calamity, she urges him on, O king of kings — "Slay the killer of my husband!" — thus again and again, O subduer of foes.

**2.13.47**  Then, O great king, remembering that counsel we had deliberated before, we departed in distress, O lord of men.

**2.13.48**  We fled separately, O king, abandoning our great prosperity — fleeing in fear of him, along with our wealth, kinsmen, and relations.

**2.13.49**  Thinking thus, we all sought the western direction and the lovely city of Kuśasthalī, beautified by Mount Raivataka.

**2.13.50**  We established our dwelling there again, O king, and likewise fortified it in a manner difficult to approach even by the gods.

**2.13.51**  In that city even women could fight — how much more the foremost of Vṛṣṇis! We dwell there, O slayer of foes, without fear from any quarter.

**2.13.52**  Beholding that foremost of mountains and the tīrtha sacred to the Mādhavīs, the Mādhavakas, O tiger among Kurus, attained the highest joy.

**2.13.53**  Thus we, originally wronged by Jarāsandha, though possessed of power, have through kinship sought refuge with you.

**2.13.54**  His abode extends three yojanas, is three-storied and more than a yojana high, has a hundred gates at each yojana's end, with successive arches of might, and is garrisoned by eighteen ranks of kṣatriyas frenzied for battle.

**2.13.55**  Eighteen thousand warriors of our clan there are; Āhuka has a hundred sons, each attended by no fewer than three hundred.

**2.13.56**  Cārudeṣṇa with his brother, Cakradeva, and Sātyaki; and I, and Rauhiṇeya, and Sāmba — equal to Śauri in battle —

**2.13.57**  These seven chariot-warriors, O king — now hear of others from me: Kṛtavarmā, Anādhṛṣṭi, Samīka, and Samitiṃjaya —

**2.13.58**  Kahva, Śaṅku, and Nidānta — these seven too are great chariot-warriors; the two sons of the Andhaka-Bhoja and the old king — those ten.

**2.13.59**  Heroes built like the pillars of the world, valiant and greatly powerful — remembering the middle country, they are afflicted with grief even among the Vṛṣṇis.

**2.13.60**  You, endowed with the qualities of an emperor, O best among Bharatas, you deserve to make yourself sovereign over the kṣatriyas, O Bhārata.

**2.13.61**  But it is not possible for you to accomplish the Rājasūya while Jarāsandha of great power still lives — this is my view, O king.

**2.13.62**  For by him all those kings have been imprisoned after being conquered at Girivraja — in the mountain cave, like great elephants by a lion.

**2.13.63**  That king Jarāsandha too, wishing to sacrifice with the lords of the earth, propitiated Mahādeva — and those kings were conquered by him.

**2.13.64**  For he, conquering again and again the kings who came with their armies, brought them to his city, bound them, and made a pen of men.

**2.13.65**  And we too, O great king, out of fear of Jarāsandha at that time abandoned Mathurā and went to the city of Dvāravatī.
