**2.13.66**  But if, O great king, you wish to attain this sacrifice, then strive for the liberation of those kings and for the slaying of Jarāsandha.

**2.13.67**  For this undertaking is possible in no other way, O joy of the Kurus — to fully perform the Rājasūya, O best of the wise.

**2.13.68**  This is my view, O king — or as you yourself think best, O sinless one, in this situation, declare it, having resolved it with your own reasoning.

**2.14.1**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: What you have spoken, O wise one, no other is worthy to speak — for there is no other on earth who can resolve doubts as you can.

**2.14.2**  In every household kings perform what is pleasing to themselves — but they have not attained sovereignty, for the title "emperor" encompasses all.

**2.14.3**  How can one who knows the greatness of others praise himself? He who is praised in the company of his peers — he is truly honored.

**2.14.4**  The earth is vast and abundant, filled with many jewels — one learns what is best by going far, O chief of the Vṛṣṇi clan.

**2.14.5**  I consider tranquility to be supreme — yet there is no tranquility apart from liberation. But sovereignty through undertaking is not to be attained — this is my view.

**2.14.6**  Thus those of noble birth who are high-minded understand — among them, someone at some time may become the foremost, O Janārdana.

**2.14.7**  Bhīma said: A king devoted to inaction sinks like an anthill. And he who faces a powerful enemy without proper means is weak indeed.

**2.14.8**  Yet the vigilant one, though weak, can generally defeat a powerful enemy — through right policy, O king, understanding the ends of statecraft for his own benefit.

**2.14.9**  In Kṛṣṇa is wisdom, in me is strength, victory is in Pārtha Dhanaṃjaya — we three shall accomplish the Māgadha's defeat, like the three fires.

**2.14.10**  Kṛṣṇa said: A foolish man intent on gain does not consider the consequences — therefore those who are intent on gain do not tolerate an enemy who is foolish.

**2.14.11**  Yauvanāśva through renouncing tributes, Bhagīratha through protection, Kārtavīrya through the practice of austerities, the mighty Bharata through strength, and Marutta through prosperity — these five we have heard of as emperors.

**2.14.12**  Jarāsandha, son of Bṛhadratha, has acquired the marks of one to be subdued as indicated by the marks of dharma, artha, and policy — know this, O bull among Bharatas.

**2.14.13**  And one hundred and one clans of kings do not follow him willingly — therefore he now maintains this sovereignty by force alone.

**2.14.14**  Kings who share in his treasury attend upon Jarāsandha — yet he is not satisfied even by that, having adopted a wrong course through childishness.

**2.14.15**  He seizes by force a lord of kings, a man of the highest rank — and nowhere have we seen a portion taken from a man without consequence.

**2.14.16**  Thus Jarāsandha has brought under his control those ranked below by the hundred — how then shall a king weaker than he approach him, O Pārtha?

**2.14.17**  For kings who have been sprinkled and anointed as victims in the house of the lord of creatures — what joy is there in life, like animals consecrated for sacrifice, O bull among Bharatas?

**2.14.18**  When a kṣatriya dies by the sword, he is honored — should we not all therefore repel the Māgadha king?

**2.14.19**  Eighty-six kings have been gathered; the remaining fourteen, O king — when Jarāsandha has them too, something terrible will come to pass.

**2.14.20**  He would attain blazing glory — he who creates an obstacle there, and he who conquers Jarāsandha shall certainly become emperor.

**2.15.1**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Desiring the quality of sovereignty and being devoted to my own interests, how could I send Bhīma through sheer recklessness and brute force?

**2.15.2**  I consider both Bhīma and Arjuna to be my eyes, and Janārdana to be my mind — what would life be for one deprived of mind and eyes?

**2.15.3**  Encountering the force of Jarāsandha, which is hard to cross and of terrible might — your exertion would end in defeat; what then could be accomplished there?

**2.15.4**  In this matter there lies another consideration where misfortune may result. As I alone reflect on it, let this be heard from me now.

**2.15.5**  I favour renunciation of this enterprise, O Janardana. The Rajasuya sacrifice, so difficult to achieve, weighs heavily upon my mind today.

**2.15.6**  Vaishampayana said: Partha, having obtained the finest bow, two inexhaustible quivers, a chariot, a banner, and the assembly hall, then spoke to Yudhishthira.

**2.15.7**  O King, I have obtained this bow, weapons, arrows, prowess, allies, land, glory, and strength — all that was desired and hard to attain.

**2.15.8**  The learned who are well established rightly praise noble birth; yet there is nothing equal to strength, and prowess is what appeals to me.

**2.15.9**  What will a kshatriya born in the lineage of Kritavirya accomplish if he is without prowess, O King — one whose livelihood consists in defeat?

**2.15.10**  Even one lacking all other qualities, if he possesses prowess, can overcome enemies; but what can one endowed with all qualities accomplish if he lacks prowess?

**2.15.11**  All qualities reside as substance in courage. Victory depends on accomplishment, and accomplishment in turn rests on action and fate.

**2.15.12**  One who is endowed with forces but does not employ them through negligence — through that very negligence, the powerful enemy is weakened.

**2.15.13**  Just as despondency is ruinous in the weak, so delusion is ruinous in the strong. Both these destructive causes must be abandoned by a king who seeks victory.

**2.15.14**  If we were to destroy Jarasandha and free the imprisoned kings for the sake of the sacrifice, what greater achievement could there be?

**2.15.15**  In non-undertaking, the certainty of failure is assured. When the qualities are beyond doubt, O King, how can you think there is a lack of merit?

**2.15.16**  The ochre robe is easily donned later by sages seeking tranquility; but desiring sovereignty for you, we shall fight the enemy.

**2.16.1**  Vasudeva said: Born in the lineage of Bharata and as the son of Kunti, the fitting counsel has been shown by Arjuna.

**2.16.2**  We do not know the appointed time of death — whether by night or by day. Nor have we heard of anyone who is immortal even without fighting.

**2.16.3**  This much alone is what brings true satisfaction to men: to attack enemies by means of wise policy prescribed by scripture.

**2.16.4**  In battle, the highest course for sound and unfailing policy is this: when forces are equal, doubt arises; yet equality between the two parties is never truly so.

**2.16.5**  We, relying on good counsel and approaching near the enemy's person, how can we not bring him to his end — as rivers erode trees — attacking his weaknesses while guarding our own?

**2.16.6**  That one should not advance with arrayed but insufficient forces against a mightier foe — this is the policy of the wise, and I too approve of it here.

**2.16.7**  Those who enter the enemy's abode undetected and blameless, attacking the enemy in person, shall accomplish that which they desire.

**2.16.8**  One man alone ever bears the fortune, O bull among men, as the inner self sustains all beings; when he is destroyed, the strength of the army is destroyed.

**2.16.9**  And if, having slain him in battle, we proceed together with the remainder of the army, we shall attain heaven, devoted as we are to the protection of our kinsmen.

**2.16.10**  Yudhishthira said: O Krishna, who is this Jarasandha? What is his valour, what his prowess — he who, having touched you who are like fire, was not burned like a moth?

**2.16.11**  Krishna said: Hear, O King, what valour and prowess Jarasandha possesses, and why, though he repeatedly wronged us, he was left unchecked by us.

**2.16.12**  There was a king, proud in battle, named Brihadratha, lord among lords of Magadha, who commanded three akshauhinis of troops.

**2.16.13**  He was handsome, endowed with prowess, glorious, of incomparable valour, his body always lean from ritual observances — like a second Indra of a hundred sacrifices.

**2.16.14**  In splendour he was like the sun, in forbearance like the earth, in anger like Yama and Death together, in prosperity like Vaishravana.

**2.16.15**  With his virtues combined with noble lineage, O best of the Bharatas, the entire earth was pervaded, as by the rays of the sun.

**2.16.16**  That mighty hero, O bull of the Bharatas, married the two twin daughters of the king of Kashi, who were equal in beauty and wealth.

**2.16.17**  That bull among men made a compact between them, saying, "I shall not transgress either of you," in the presence of both his wives.

**2.16.18**  That king, O lord of men, shone magnificently between his two beloved, well-matched wives, like an elephant between two she-elephants.

**2.16.19**  That lord of the earth, standing between the two of them, shone like the ocean in embodied form between the Ganga and the Yamuna.

**2.16.20**  His youth passed while he was immersed in the pleasures of the senses, yet no son was born to him to perpetuate his lineage.

**2.16.21**  Through many auspicious fire-offerings and sacrifices desired by those longing for sons, that best of kings still did not obtain a son to further his family.

**2.16.22**  Then he heard that Chandakaushika, the noble son of Kakshivat of the great-souled Gautama lineage, was exhausted from austerities nearby.

**2.16.23**  The king, accompanied by his wives, gratified with all kinds of precious gifts that sage who had come by chance and was resting at the foot of a tree.

**2.16.24**  That best of sages, firm in truth and true in speech, said to him: "I am well pleased with you, O King. Choose a boon, O man of good vows."

**2.16.25**  Then Brihadratha, bowing with his wives, spoke to him in a voice choked with tears, despairing of ever seeing a son.

**2.16.26**  Brihadratha said: O Blessed One, I who am of little fortune am setting out to the forest, abandoning my kingdom. What use is a boon to me? What use is a kingdom to one without a son?

**2.16.27**  Krishna said: Hearing this, the sage fell into deep meditation, his senses stirred, and he sat down in the shade of that very mango tree.

**2.16.28**  As the sage sat there, there fell into his lap a single mango fruit, windfall-ripe and bitten by a parrot.

**2.16.29**  The best of sages took that fruit, consecrated it with his heart's power, and gave to the king that unequalled fruit which would bring the attainment of a son.

**2.16.30**  The great sage of vast wisdom said to the king: "Go, O King, your purpose is accomplished. Turn back, O lord of men."

**2.16.31**  Then that best of kings, understanding the instruction in accordance with the compact he had made, gave that one fruit to both his wives, O bull of the Bharatas.

**2.16.32**  Those two auspicious queens cut that mango in two and ate it — both by reason of what was destined and also by the truth of the sage's word.

**2.16.33**  A pregnancy arose in each of them from eating the fruit, and the king, seeing this, was filled with the highest joy.

**2.16.34**  Then, O greatly wise one, when the time arrived, O King, both of them gave birth — each to a half of a body.

**2.16.35**  Seeing those halves of a body — each with one eye, one arm, one leg, half an abdomen, half a face, half a hip — both women trembled greatly.

**2.16.36**  Those two anxious sisters consulted together, and in their great sorrow abandoned the still-living, breathing halves.

**2.16.37**  The two nurses of those queens, having well wrapped those products of birth, went out through the inner palace gate and quickly cast them away.

**2.16.38**  Those halves, cast at the crossroads, were then seized by a rakshasi named Jara, O tiger among men — one who feeds on flesh and blood.

**2.16.39**  Intending to consume those easy-to-carry halves, that rakshasi joined them together, driven by the force of fate.

**2.16.40**  The moment those halves were brought together, O bull among men, and made into one form, a mighty boy came into being.

**2.16.41**  Then that rakshasi, O King, her eyes wide with wonder, was unable to lift that infant whose very substance was like adamant.

**2.16.42**  The boy clenched his copper-hued fist, placed it in his mouth, and cried out with great fury like a rain-cloud full of water.

**2.16.43**  At that sound the people of the inner palace were suddenly startled and rushed out, O tiger among men, together with the king, O scorcher of foes.

**2.16.44**  And those two exhausted women with their milk-filled breasts, who had despaired of obtaining a son, suddenly came forward as well.

**2.16.45**  Then seeing the king in that condition, his continued activity, and that very powerful boy, the rakshasi reflected.

**2.16.46**  "Living within the domain of a king who yearns for a son, I ought not to take away this infant son, as a streak of cloud cannot cover the sun."

**2.16.47**  She assumed a human form and said to the lord of men: "O Brihadratha, this is your son — given by me. Please accept him."

**2.16.48**  "Born of your two wives through the command of the best among the twice-born, abandoned by the nurses, he was protected by me."

**2.16.49**  Then, O best of Bharatas, those two auspicious daughters of the king of Kashi quickly rushed to that boy and bathed him with streams of milk.

**2.16.50**  Then that king, filled with joy and having understood everything, questioned that rakshasi who appeared like fresh gold and unlike a rakshasi at all.

**2.16.51**  "Who are you, O one radiant as the heart of a lotus, who has given me a son? Speak freely, O auspicious one — you appear to me as a goddess."

**2.17.1**  The rakshasi said: I am a rakshasi named Jara, O blessings to you, one who assumes forms at will. I dwelt happily in your household, O king of kings, being honoured there.

**2.17.2**  Always thinking of how to return your kindness, O King, I, ever meditating on it, found these two half-portions of your son, O righteous one.

**2.17.3**  When joined by me, by the will of fate a boy came into being. It is by your own fortune, O great king; I was merely the instrument here.

**2.17.4**  Krishna said: Having spoken thus, O King, she vanished on the spot. The king, taking that boy, entered his own home.

**2.17.5**  The king performed all the rites proper for that child, and in honour of the rakshasi he ordered a great festival throughout Magadha.

**2.17.6**  His father, equal to Prajapati, there performed the naming ceremony for him. Because he had been joined together by Jara, he thus became known as Jarasandha — "joined by Jara."

**2.17.7**  That son of the lord of Magadha, of great splendour, grew up, endowed with perfect strength, like a fire fed with oblations.

**2.17.8**  Then after some time, the blessed Chandakaushika, the great ascetic, again came to Magadha.

**2.17.9**  Delighted at his arrival, Brihadratha went out with his ministers, his retinue, his wives, and his son.

**2.17.10**  That king honoured him with water for the feet, the guest-offering, and water for sipping, O Bharata, and presented his son along with the kingdom to the sage.

**2.17.11**  The blessed sage, having accepted that honour from the king, spoke to the Magadha king with a soul rejoicing within.

**2.17.12**  "O King, all of this has been known to me through the eye of wisdom. But hear, O king of kings, what this son of yours shall become."

**2.17.13**  "The prowess of this mighty one shall not be matched by kings. Even weapons hurled by gods, O lord of the earth, shall not wound him — like rivers against a mountain."

**2.17.14**  "He shall blaze at the crown of all consecrated kings, dimming the splendour of them all, as the sun dims all other luminaries."

**2.17.15**  "Kings with prosperous forces and armies, encountering him, shall come to destruction — like moths meeting fire."

**2.17.16**  "He shall seize the accumulated glory of all kings, as in the rainy season the ocean — lord of rivers — swallows the high-watered rivers."

**2.17.17**  This mighty one shall rightly uphold the four social orders, even as the earth — abundant and bearing all crops — sustains both the auspicious and the inauspicious.

**2.17.18**  All kings shall come under his command, just as embodied beings are subject to the wind, which is the very self of all creatures.

**2.17.19**  This lord of Magadha, surpassing all in strength throughout the worlds, shall behold with his own eyes Rudra — the great god Hara, the destroyer of the triple city.

**2.17.20**  Speaking thus and reflecting upon his own purpose, the sage dismissed king Brihadratha, O slayer of foes.

**2.17.21**  Returning to the city surrounded by kinsmen and relatives, the lord of Magadha, king Brihadratha, then consecrated Jarasandha to the throne and attained supreme contentment.

**2.17.22**  Once Jarasandha had been consecrated, king Brihadratha, accompanied by his two wives, devoted himself to the life of the forest-hermitage.

**2.17.23**  While his father dwelt in the forest-hermitage together with his mothers, O Bharata, Jarasandha by his own valor brought the kings of the earth under his dominion.

**2.17.24**  Then, after a long time, king Brihadratha, dwelling in the hermitage and having practiced austerities, departed for heaven together with his wives.

**2.17.25**  He had two ministers — Hamsa and Dibhaka — both of whom were destined to die without weapons, foremost among the wise in counsel, and skilled in the science of warfare.

**2.17.26**  Those two mighty ones of whom I spoke to you earlier — together with Jarasandha, the three of them were, in my judgment, a match for the three worlds.

**2.17.27**  Thus was he, O hero and great king, left unchallenged at that time by the mighty Kukuras, Andhakas, and Vrishnis — for reasons of statecraft.

**2.18.1**  Vasudeva said: With Hamsa and Dibhaka — the ministers of Kamsa — slain and brought low, the time for Jarasandha's destruction has now arrived.

**2.18.2**  He cannot be conquered in open battle even by all the gods and demons together — we understand that he can only be overcome in a fight to the death, hand to hand.

**2.18.3**  In me resides policy; in Bhima, strength; and Arjuna is the protector of us both. Together we shall accomplish this, O king — like the three sacred fires.

**2.18.4**  When that lord of men is confronted by three of us in a secluded place, there is no doubt he will accept to fight with only one of us.

**2.18.5**  Stung by the contempt shown before the world and provoked by Bhimasena, he will certainly come forward to fight.

**2.18.6**  The long-armed Bhimasena of great strength is equal to destroying him — like Death come to end the life of one whose time has run out.

**2.18.7**  If your heart knows this and if you have trust in me, then quickly entrust Bhimasena and Arjuna to me as a sacred charge.

**2.18.8**  Vaishampayana said: Thus addressed by the blessed one, Yudhishthira replied, casting a glance at Bhima and Partha who stood there with joyful faces.

**2.18.9**  O Achyuta, O Achyuta — do not speak thus, O crusher of enemies! You are the lord and refuge of the Pandavas, and we take shelter in you.

**2.18.10**  Whatever you say, O Govinda, comes to pass — for fortune never turns her back on those who have you at their front.

**2.18.11**  Jarasandha is as good as slain, the captive kings as good as freed, and the Rajasuya sacrifice as good as mine — for I stand under your direction.

**2.18.12**  Act swiftly so that this task may be accomplished — my task which is also the task of the world. Do this, O best of men.

**2.18.13**  Without the three of you I cannot bear to live — like one in misery who is afflicted by disease and bereft of dharma, desire, and wealth.

**2.18.14**  Partha is nothing without Shauri, and Shauri nothing without the Pandava — in this world none can conquer either of the two Krishnas, so I believe.

**2.18.15**  And this glorious Vrikodara, foremost among the strong, joined together with both of you — what could that illustrious hero not accomplish?

**2.18.16**  A mass of strength well directed accomplishes the highest deeds — raw strength without guidance is called blind and dull; it must be led by the wise.

**2.18.17**  Water flows wherever there is a downward slope; those rich in wisdom likewise direct an army wherever there is an opening.

**2.18.18**  Therefore, taking refuge in Govinda — a man famed throughout the world and learned in the ways of statecraft — let us strive for the accomplishment of our purpose.

**2.18.19**  Thus one who combines wisdom, statecraft, and strength, and is furnished with the right means of action — place such a one, O Krishna, in the forefront of all undertakings for the accomplishment of our aim.

**2.18.20**  Even so, let Arjuna follow Krishna, the foremost of the Yadus, for the accomplishment of the purpose, and let Bhima follow Dhananjaya — through policy, victory, and strength combined, success shall be attained in the campaign.

**2.18.21**  Thus instructed, all those brothers of vast prowess — the scion of the Vrishnis and the two Pandava heroes — then set out toward Magadha.

**2.18.22**  Clad in the garments of radiant Brahmin graduates, they were bid farewell with affectionate and pleasing words by their friends.

**2.18.23**  Wearing the chief garments of those who burn with indignation for the sake of their kinsmen — those whose forms blaze like the sun, moon, and fire — the appearance they presented then was awesome.

**2.18.24**  Seeing the two Krishnas — unconquered in battle, united in purpose, and with Bhima leading the way — one would have reckoned Jarasandha already slain.

**2.18.25**  For those two great-souled lords were capable of setting in motion any enterprise, just as they were capable of suppressing all matters pertaining to dharma, artha, and kama.

**2.18.26**  Departing from the Kurus, they traveled through the heart of the Kurujangala region, visiting the lovely Padmasara lake and passing beyond Kalakuta.

**2.18.27**  They crossed the Gandaki, then the Shona, then the Sadanira, and likewise the rivers near Ekaparvataka — proceeding onward one by one.

**2.18.28**  Fording the lovely Sarayu and beholding the eastern Kosalas, they passed through Mithila, and the river Mala, and the Charmanvati.

**2.18.29**  Having crossed the Ganga and the Shona, all three of them — those unfailing heroes — proceeded eastward through the Kuraura forest into the territory of Magadha.

**2.18.30**  Reaching mount Goratha — ever filled with herds of cattle, rich with water, and adorned with beautiful trees — they beheld the city of Magadha.

**2.19.1**  Vasudeva said: O Partha, this Magadhan settlement is great, pleasant, ever well-stocked with cattle, rich in water, free from illness, furnished with fine dwellings, and auspicious.

**2.19.2**  There is the great Vaihara mountain, and Varaha, and Vrishabha, and likewise Rishigiri, O dear one — and the auspicious Chaityaka is the fifth of these.

**2.19.3**  These five great-peaked mountains, shaded by cool trees, seem to stand united in body, guarding Girivraja together in a compact embrace.

**2.19.4**  With fragrant and charming trees whose branch-tips are wreathed with blossoms, the city lies concealed as if within the groves of lodhra trees so beloved by lovers.

**2.19.5**  Here the great-souled sage Gautama, firm in his vows, fathered Kakshivat and other sons — upon a Shudra woman and upon a woman of the Ushinara clan.

**2.19.6**  Gautama, descended from this lineage, still dwells in the hermitage there and is connected to the Magadhan royal line by the grace of its kings.

**2.19.7**  Kings of great strength — the rulers of Anga, Vanga, and others — used to come to Gautama's hermitage and take their pleasure there in former times, O Arjuna.

**2.19.8**  Look at these rows of trees — the lovely priyala trees and the auspicious lodhra trees, O Partha — growing near the dwelling of Gautama.

**2.19.9**  Here are Arbuda and Shakravapi, two serpents who torment enemies; here too is the abode of Svastika, and the excellent dwelling of Maninaga.

**2.19.10**  The clouds cannot avoid shedding rain on the Magadhan land for the sake of this jewel-serpent; both Kaushika and Maninaga have grown in power by his favor.

**2.19.11**  Jarasandha considers his prosperity to be unassailable — but today we shall strike down his pride by confronting him.

**2.19.12**  Vaishampayana said: Having spoken thus, all those brothers of vast prowess — the scion of the Vrishnis and the two Pandavas — then set out toward the city of Magadha.

**2.19.13**  They reached Girivraja — filled with contented and well-fed people, crowded with the four social orders, abounding in festivals, and impregnable.

**2.19.14**  Without approaching the gate, they made for the lofty mountain-range of the city — which was honored by the descendants of Brihadratha and by the city's inhabitants alike.

**2.19.15**  It was there that Brihadratha had encountered the bull that fed on beans; he slew it, had three great drums made from its hide and stalk-sinew,

**2.19.16**  And, having covered them with its hide, installed them in his city — where those drums resounded, sprinkled with divine flowers.

**2.19.17**  Those who sought to slay Jarasandha rushed upon the lovely Chaityaka peak of the Magadhans — as if intending to cut off a head.

**2.19.18**  It was a firm and very broad peak — vast and ancient, ever worshipped with garlands and wreaths, well established and revered.

**2.19.19**  The heroes shattered it with their powerful arms and brought it down; then, beholding the city of Magadha, they entered it.

**2.19.20**  At that very moment the royal priests were performing rites of honor for Jarasandha and leading the king, mounted on an elephant, around the sacred fire.

**2.19.21**  Those three, observing the vows of Brahmin graduates — with their arms as their only weapons, bearing no other armament, and eager to fight — entered together with Jarasandha, O Bharata.

**2.19.22**  They beheld the supreme splendor of the food-stalls and flower-shops — prosperous, endowed with every good quality, and abounding in all desirable things.

**2.19.23**  Seeing this prosperity, those foremost of men — Krishna, Bhima, and Dhananjaya — proceeded along the royal road through that market-lane.

**2.19.24**  Those mighty men forcibly took garlands from the garland-sellers; all of them clad in faded garments, wearing flower-wreaths and gleaming earrings.

**2.19.25**  They then approached the dwelling of the wise Jarasandha, gazing about as if surveying a cow-pasture — like lions from the Himalayan slopes.

**2.19.26**  Their arms — anointed with sandal and aloe-wood, resembling stone pillars — shone brilliantly, O great king, those arms of those mighty-armed heroes.

**2.19.27**  Seeing them — broad-chested, resembling elephants, rising like the trunks of sala trees — amazement arose among the Magadhans.

**2.19.28**  Those foremost of men, of great strength, passed through three crowd-filled outer enclosures and then approached the king with self-assurance.

**2.19.29**  Jarasandha rose to receive them — for they were worthy of the water-offering and the honey-gift, worthy of honor, and deserving of respectful welcome — and attended to them according to the prescribed rites.

**2.19.30**  That lord and king said to them: "Welcome to you all." For this was a vow of his, O king — widely renowned throughout the earth.

**2.19.31**  Even at midnight, O Bharata, that conqueror of assemblies — that king — would go forth to receive Brahmin graduates who had arrived.

**2.19.32**  But seeing them in their unusual attire, the foremost of kings — Jarasandha — attended to them and was filled with wonder.

**2.19.33**  But those foremost of men — slayers of enemies all — upon seeing king Jarasandha, spoke thus, O best of the Bharatas.

**2.19.34**  "May it go well with you, O king — be in good health" — so they all said as they stood there; then, O tiger among kings, they gazed at each other and at that king.

**2.19.35**  Then Jarasandha addressed those Yadavas and Pandavas — concealed beneath the disguise of Brahmins — saying: "Be seated, O lords."

**2.19.36**  Then all three of those foremost of men took their seats — blazing with splendor, the three of them like the three sacred fires at a great sacrifice.

**2.19.37**  Then Jarasandha — that truthful lord of men — addressed them, reproaching them on account of the reason for their disguise, O Kauravya.

**2.19.38**  "It is known to me in every way, in this world of men, that Brahmins who observe the vows of graduates do not wear their garlands and unguents on the outside of their garments."

**2.19.39**  "Yet you wear flowers, and you bear the marks of bowstrings on your arms — you carry the energy of the warrior class, yet you claim to be Brahmins."

**2.19.40**  Dressed in colourless garments, without garlands or ointments — speak the truth: who are you? Truth is what adorns men among kings.

**2.19.41**  You have broken through the Caityaka peak of the mountain and entered our abode without using a gate, fearless of any offence to the king — what accounts for this?

**2.19.42**  This is the act of one who bears a disguise. What purpose did you have in view? Declare your vigour in speech — especially, what distinguishes a brahmin?

**2.19.43**  And having thus approached me, why do you not accept the ceremonial welcome offered according to custom? What is your purpose in coming to me?

**2.19.44**  Thus addressed, Kṛṣṇa of great spirit — eloquent and skilled in discourse — replied in a voice at once warm and grave.

**2.19.45**  O king, those who follow the snātaka vow — brahmin, kṣatriya, and vaiśya — have rules both particular to each order and rules they share in common.

**2.19.46**  The kṣatriya in particular constantly worships Śrī with distinction; and Śrī abides unfailingly where flowers bloom — therefore we wear flowers.

**2.19.47**  A kṣatriya's valour resides in his arms, not in his words; his speech is therefore restrained — that is the rule remembered, O son of Bṛhadratha.

**2.19.48**  The Creator has placed the kṣatriya's true strength in his two arms. If you wish to see it, O king, you shall see it this very day — doubt it not.

**2.19.49**  The righteous always enter an enemy's house by a way other than the door, and enter a friend's house through the proper gate. Therefore we avoided the gate — you are our enemy.

**2.19.50**  Coming to the house of an enemy on a mission, we do not accept ceremonial honour — know this to be our eternal vow.

**2.20.1**  Jarāsaṃdha said: I cannot recall any enmity that you have created, and even on reflection I see no wrong done to me by you.

**2.20.2**  If there is no wrong, how do you regard me as an enemy? Declare that openly, O brahmins — for this is the compact of the righteous.

**2.20.3**  Indeed, the mind of one who attacks the guiltless is tormented by the destruction of dharma — even if he be a kṣatriya, there is no doubt.

**2.20.4**  One who knows dharma and holds great vows, yet acts otherwise in the world, attains the fate of sinners and destroys whatever good he had earned.

**2.20.5**  Knowing that the guiltless are the best among those who walk rightly through the three worlds by the dharma of the kṣatriya, you speak as though in delusion.

**2.20.6**  Vāsudeva said: O great king, the duty of a family is borne by one upholder of that family; yet by its commission we three have risen to this task.

**2.20.7**  O king, you have seized kṣatriyas who dwell in the world — committing thereby a cruel transgression — yet you consider yourself guiltless?

**2.20.8**  O best of kings, how can a king harm righteous kings? You are seizing those kings in order to offer them to Rudra.

**2.20.9**  That sin committed by you, O son of Bṛhadratha, would fall upon us — for we who walk in dharma are capable of protecting it.

**2.20.10**  The seizure of human beings as sacrificial victims has never been sanctioned. How then do you wish to worship the god Śaṃkara with human victims?

**2.20.11**  Who else would treat men of his own order as sacrificial animals? Only you, Jarāsaṃdha, with your perverted mind.

**2.20.12**  We, who follow those in distress, have come here to restrain you — you who cause the destruction of kinsmen — for the sake of the welfare and growth of our kinsmen.

**2.20.13**  Your belief that there is no other man among the kṣatriyas of the world equal to you — that too, O king, is a very great confusion of your mind.

**2.20.14**  What kṣatriya who knows his own lineage would not enter the incomparable, imperishable heaven that follows upon battle, O king?

**2.20.15**  Consecrated to the sacrifice of battle and ascending to heaven itself, kṣatriyas worship the worlds thereby — know this, O lord of Magadha.

**2.20.16**  Victory is the source of heaven, O king; great glory is the source of heaven; austerity in battle is the source of heaven — this path does not deviate.

**2.20.17**  This is the victorious quality of Indra, ever present and focused, by which the Hundred-Sacrificed one conquered the asuras and protects the world.

**2.20.18**  Who would be so bellicose as you, relying on heaven, proud of the abundance and strength of your vast Magadhan armies?

**2.20.19**  Do not despise others, O king — valour is not equal in every man; the same energy is not found in any man as in you alone, O lord of men.

**2.20.20**  Only as long as it is not fully understood would this be endurable for you. Therefore, O king, I tell you this.

**2.20.21**  Abandon your pride and arrogance among equals, O Māgadha — do not go with your sons, ministers, and army to the abode of Yama.

**2.20.22**  Dambhodbhava, Kārtavīrya, and Uttara Bṛhadratha — these kings who despised their betters perished here along with their armies.

**2.20.23**  Seeking to free those kings from you, we are not those who merely call themselves brahmins — I am Śauri, Hṛṣīkeśa, and these two are the hero Pāṇḍava brothers.

**2.20.24**  We challenge you, O king — stand firm and fight, O Māgadha, or release all the kings; do not go to the realm of Yama.

**2.20.25**  Jarāsaṃdha said: I do not take any kings who are not already conquered. Who has stood against me? Who is there that I have not conquered?

**2.20.26**  This is what they call the righteous livelihood of the kṣatriya, O Kṛṣṇa: to subdue by valour and bring under one's sway, then to do as one pleases.

**2.20.27**  Having dedicated these kings for the sake of the deity, O Kṛṣṇa, how should I release them out of fear today, mindful as I am of the kṣatriya vow?

**2.20.28**  I shall fight army against army in battle array, or one against one again, or against two or three — simultaneously or one by one.

**2.20.29**  Vaiśampāyana said: Having spoken thus, Jarāsaṃdha the king, eager for battle and mighty in deed, then commanded the consecration of Sahadeva.

**2.20.30**  That king, as battle drew near, O bull of the Bharatas, remembered Kauśika and Citrasena as his commanders.

**2.20.31**  Those two whose names — Haṃsa and Ḍibhaka — had long been spoken of among men in the world, honoured by the world.

**2.20.32**  But the mighty Śauri, O king, O tiger among men, remembering that king — best among the strong, his valour like a tiger's —

**2.20.33**  — true to his pledge, Jarāsaṃdha of fearful prowess on earth, assigned as the portion of another to be slain — Acyuta, sustainer of the earth,

**2.20.34**  — Madhusūdana, the younger brother of the wielder of the plough, the foremost among the self-possessed, did not wish to slay him by his own hand; placing the command of Brahmā first, he deferred the act.

**2.21.1**  Vaiśampāyana said: Then the eloquent Adhokṣaja, the delight of the Yadus, spoke to King Jarāsaṃdha, whose mind was resolute for battle.

**2.21.2**  Of the three of us, O king, with which does your mind incline to fight? Let whichever one of us you choose make himself ready for combat.

**2.21.3**  Thus addressed by Kṛṣṇa, the Māgadha, Jarāsaṃdha of great splendour, chose to fight with Bhīmasena, O king.

**2.21.4**  Bearing the choicest remedies, curative preparations, and medicinal herbs, the royal priest approached Jarāsaṃdha who was eager to fight.

**2.21.5**  Having performed the rite of benediction through a learned and distinguished brahmin, Jarāsaṃdha — devoted to the duty of the kṣatriya — girded himself.

**2.21.6**  Removing his crown and smoothing his hair, Jarāsaṃdha rose up like an ocean overflowing its shore.

**2.21.7**  The wise king said to Bhīma of fearful prowess: Bhīma, I shall fight you — it is better to be defeated by a superior than otherwise.

**2.21.8**  Having spoken thus, Jarāsaṃdha the enemy-subduer, of great energy, advanced towards Bhīmasena as the asura Bali once advanced against Indra.

**2.21.9**  Then, having taken counsel with Kṛṣṇa and performed the rite of benediction, the mighty Bhīmasena approached Jarāsaṃdha with eagerness to fight.

**2.21.10**  Then those two tigers among men, their arms their weapons, met — both heroes supremely exhilarated, each longing for victory over the other.

**2.21.11**  From the blows of their arms, from restraints and releases, there arose a tremendous crash like that of thunderbolt against mountain.

**2.21.12**  Both supremely elated, both exceeding strong, each seeking an opening in the other, each desiring victory over the other.

**2.21.13**  That dreadful combat, with the crowd driven back, took place in a secluded spot, O king — the clash of those two mighty ones like Vṛtra and Indra.

**2.21.14**  By pulling and dragging, by drawing close and pushing back, they hauled each other about, and struck one another with their knees.

**2.21.15**  Then, threatening each other with great shouts, they struck each other with blows resembling the collision of boulders.

**2.21.16**  Both broad-chested and long-armed, both skilled in wrestling, they locked each other with their arms like iron bars.

**2.21.17**  The combat began on the first day of the month of Kārttika and went on ceaselessly, without rest, day and night.

**2.21.18**  That engagement of the two great souls continued through the thirteenth; but on the night of the fourteenth, the Māgadha withdrew, exhausted.

**2.21.19**  Seeing the king thus exhausted, O king, Janārdana spoke to Bhīma of terrible deeds, as if awakening him.

**2.21.20**  An exhausted enemy, O son of Kuntī, cannot be pressed in battle — for one wholly pressed may abandon his own life.

**2.21.21**  Therefore, O son of Kuntī, do not press this king to the utmost — fight him evenly with your arms, O bull of the Bharatas.

**2.21.22**  Thus instructed by Kṛṣṇa, the Pāṇḍava slayer of enemy heroes, having discerned the weak point of Jarāsaṃdha, resolved upon his killing.

**2.21.23**  Then, to conquer the hitherto unconquered Jarāsaṃdha, Vṛkodara — foremost among the strong, joy of the Kurus — seized him with vigour.

**2.22.1**  Vaiśampāyana said: Then Bhīmasena spoke to Kṛṣṇa, the delight of the Yadus, having formed a vast plan with the desire to slay Jarāsaṃdha.

**2.22.2**  This wicked one, O Kṛṣṇa, O tiger among the Yadus, should not be entreated for his life by me — he whose loin cloth is now bound fast.

**2.22.3**  Thus addressed, Kṛṣṇa, that tiger among men, eager for the death of Jarāsaṃdha, replied to Vṛkodara, urging him on.

**2.22.4**  Whatever supreme divine strength you possess, O Bhīma, and whatever strength of the wind-god — show it to us now, this very day, upon Jarāsaṃdha.

**2.22.5**  Thus urged, Bhīma the enemy-subduer, of great strength, lifted the mighty Jarāsaṃdha and whirled him about, O king.

**2.22.6**  Having whirled him a hundredfold with his arms, O bull of the Bharatas, he broke his back, crumpling and crushing him, and roared aloud.

**2.22.7**  As he was being crushed and the Pāṇḍava roared, there arose a tumultuous sound that struck fear into all living beings.

**2.22.8**  All the Māgadhas trembled with fear, and the wombs of women miscarried at the roar of Bhīmasena and the cry of Jarāsaṃdha.

**2.22.9**  "Has the Himālaya split? Is the earth cracking?" — so the Māgadhas understood from the sound of Bhīmasena.

**2.22.10**  Then those tamers of enemies left the king at the gate of the royal palace as though he were asleep, casting out his lifeless body by night, and departed.

**2.22.11**  Kṛṣṇa, having yoked Jarāsaṃdha's chariot with its banner and mounted the two brothers upon it, freed the captive kinsmen.

**2.22.12**  Those lords of the earth, those kings — freed from great fear — approached Kṛṣṇa who bears jewels, who is worthy of jewels, and paid him homage.

**2.22.13**  Uninjured, well-armed, his enemies conquered, accompanied by the kings, he mounted that divine chariot and departed from Girivraja.

**2.22.14**  That chariot, famed as Sodaryavān, capable of dual combat, with Kṛṣṇa as its charioteer — terrible in appearance and unconquerable by all kings.

**2.22.15**  Mounted by warriors Bhīma and Arjuna, with Kṛṣṇa as its charioteer, that foremost chariot shone splendidly — unconquerable by all archers.

**2.22.16**  For Indra and Viṣṇu had roamed on that very chariot in the battle of the gods against the demons, known as Tārakāmaya; mounting it, Kṛṣṇa now proceeded.

**2.22.17**  Gleaming like refined gold, adorned with a garland of small bells, thundering with the roar of clouds — that victorious chariot, the slayer of foes.

**2.22.18**  With that chariot Indra had slain ninety-nine times nine of the demons; obtaining it, those bulls among men rejoiced greatly.

**2.22.19**  Then the Māgadha people, seeing the mighty-armed Kṛṣṇa together with the two brothers standing on that chariot, were struck with astonishment.

**2.22.20**  Yoked with divine horses, equal to the wind in speed, that chariot, presided over by Kṛṣṇa, shone exceedingly, O Bhārata.

**2.22.21**  The banner fixed by the gods on that foremost chariot, unimpeded, could be seen from a yojana away, glorious, blazing like Indra's rainbow.

**2.22.22**  Then Kṛṣṇa thought of Garuḍa, and Garuḍa immediately came to him; in that instant, perched on the banner, he stood like a tall sacrificial post.

**2.22.23**  With wide-open mouths and mighty roars, accompanied by spirits who dwell in banners, Garuḍa the serpent-devourer stood on that foremost chariot.

**2.22.24**  Difficult for any being to gaze upon, he blazed with surpassing brilliance, like the sun at midday encompassed by a thousand rays.

**2.22.25**  It does not catch on trees, nor is it harmed by weapons — that divine foremost banner, O king, is visible to gods and humans alike.

**2.22.26**  Mounting that divine chariot whose rumble was like a rain-cloud, the tiger among men, Acyuta, set forth together with the two Pāṇḍavas.

**2.22.27**  The chariot that King Vasu had received from Vāsava (Indra), from him Bṛhadratha obtained it, and from Bṛhadratha it had passed in succession to the king descended from Bṛhadratha — Jarāsaṃdha.

**2.22.28**  The mighty-armed, lotus-eyed one of great fame then departed and halted outside Girivraja on level ground.

**2.22.29**  There all the townspeople, with brāhmaṇas at their head, O king, came to honor him with proper rites as prescribed.

**2.22.30**  The kings, freed from captivity, worshipped Madhusūdana and spoke these soothing words to him:

**2.22.31**  "This is no wonder, O mighty-armed one, O son of Devakī — that you, endowed with the strength of Bhīma and Arjuna, uphold dharma.

**2.22.32**  That you have today rescued these kings who were sinking in the terrible lake of Jarāsaṃdha, the mire of suffering —

**2.22.33**  O Viṣṇu, of those who were crushed in the terrible mountain-fortress, by good fortune glory blazing from their liberation has been won by you, O best of men.

**2.22.34**  What shall we do, O tiger among men? Speak, O bull among men — whatever you say must be understood as already accomplished by us kings, even if it be difficult."

**2.22.35**  Hṛṣīkeśa, the great-souled one, reassuring them, said: "Yudhiṣṭhira wishes to perform the Rājasūya sacrifice."

**2.22.36**  "For him who is devoted to dharma and desires sovereignty, let all of you give your assistance for the purpose of that sacrifice."

**2.22.37**  Then those kings, O bull of the Bharatas, with satisfied minds all said "So be it" and pledged their word to that.

**2.22.38**  Those lords of the earth made the Dāśārha (Kṛṣṇa) a recipient of their jewels; out of compassion for them, Govinda accepted their gifts with some reluctance.

**2.22.39**  And Sahadeva, the great chariot-warrior son of Jarāsaṃdha, came out with his kinsmen and ministers, placing his priest before him.

**2.22.40**  Bowing low, with many jewels preceding him, Sahadeva approached Vāsudeva, the god among men.

**2.22.41**  Then Kṛṣṇa, granting him freedom from fear, thereupon consecrated the son of Jarāsaṃdha as king right there.

**2.22.42**  Having achieved accord with Kṛṣṇa and being honored by the two Pārthas, that wise king re-entered the city of Bārhadratha.

**2.22.43**  But Kṛṣṇa, the lotus-eyed, blazing with supreme splendor, taking abundant jewels, set forth together with the two Pārthas.

**2.22.44**  Arriving at Indraprastha together with the two Pāṇḍavas, Acyuta, delighted, met the Dharma-king and spoke to him.

**2.22.45**  "By good fortune the mighty Jarāsaṃdha has been felled by Bhīma, and these kings have been freed from their bonds, O best of kings.

**2.22.46**  By good fortune these two, Bhīmasena and Dhanaṃjaya, have returned safely to their own city, unhurt, O Bhārata."

**2.22.47**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, honoring Kṛṣṇa as he deserved, and embracing Bhīmasena and Arjuna, was filled with joy.

**2.22.48**  Then, with Jarāsaṃdha destroyed, Ajātaśatru, having obtained the victory accomplished by his brothers, rejoiced together with his brothers.

**2.22.49**  Meeting those kings according to their age, the Pāṇḍava honored and worshipped them duly, then dismissed those lords of men.

**2.22.50**  Dismissed by Yudhiṣṭhira, those kings, with joyful hearts, quickly departed to their own kingdoms in various conveyances.

**2.22.51**  Thus the tiger among men, the greatly wise Janārdana, caused the enemy Jarāsaṃdha to be slain through the Pāṇḍavas at that time.

**2.22.52**  Having intentionally caused Jarāsaṃdha to be slain, that subduer of foes took leave of the Dharma-king and of Pṛthā and Kṛṣṇā (Draupadī), O Bhārata.

**2.22.53**  Taking leave also of Subhadrā, Bhīmasena, Phalguna (Arjuna), the twin brothers, and Dhaumya, he set out for his own city.

**2.22.54**  On that very foremost chariot, blazing like the young sun, dispatched by the Dharma-king, he made the directions resound with its divine roar.

**2.22.55**  Then the Pāṇḍavas, with Yudhiṣṭhira at their head, O bull of the Bharatas, circumambulated Kṛṣṇa the effortless doer in a rightward direction.

**2.22.56**  Then, after the blessed Kṛṣṇa, the son of Devakī, had departed — having won a great victory, the giver of safety to kings —

**2.22.57**  Their energy further heightened by that deed, O Bhārata, the Pāṇḍavas, O king, increased the great joy of Draupadī.

**2.22.58**  In that time the king, renowned for protecting his kingdom, rightfully did what was fitting, encompassing dharma, pleasure, and wealth.

**2.23.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Pārtha, having obtained the finest bow, the inexhaustible great quivers, a chariot and banner and the assembly hall, spoke to Yudhiṣṭhira.

**2.23.2**  "O king, I have obtained bow, weapons, arrows, valor, a wing (support), land, glory, and strength — all that was desired and hard to attain has been obtained by me.

**2.23.3**  In this regard I think the fitting action is to augment this treasury; I will collect tribute from all kings, O best of kings.

**2.23.4**  I will set out for conquest in the direction protected by the god of wealth, at an auspicious tithi, muhūrta, and nakṣatra.

**2.23.5**  Hearing the words of Dhanaṃjaya, Dharmarāja Yudhiṣṭhira replied to him in a voice both affectionate and deep.

**2.23.6**  "Have the worthy brāhmaṇas pronounce blessings and set forth, O bull of the Bharatas — for the sorrow of enemies and the joy of friends. Victory is certain for you, O Pārtha; obtain what you cherish and desire."

**2.23.7**  Thus addressed, Pārtha set forth, surrounded by a great army, on the divine chariot Agnidatta, which performed wondrous deeds.

**2.23.8**  Likewise Bhīmasena and the twin brothers, those bulls among men, all set out with their armies, duly honored by the Dharma-king.

**2.23.9**  The son of Pākaśāsana (Arjuna) conquered the direction beloved of the lord of wealth (the north); Bhīmasena likewise the east, and Sahadeva the south.

**2.23.10**  Nakula, skilled in weapons, conquered the western direction, O king; Dharmarāja Yudhiṣṭhira remained dwelling at Khāṇḍavaprastha.

**2.23.11**  Janamejaya said: O brāhmaṇa, narrate in detail the conquest of the directions; I am not satisfied hearing of the great deeds of my ancestors.

**2.23.12**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: I will narrate to you the victory of Dhanaṃjaya first; for the earth was conquered by the Pārthas simultaneously.

**2.23.13**  First, in the territory of the Kuṇindas, the mighty-armed Dhanaṃjaya brought the kings under his sway without excessively fierce fighting.

**2.23.14**  Conquering the Ānartās, the Kālakūṭas, and the Kuṇindas, he made Sumaṇḍala and Pāpajit followers with their armies.

**2.23.15**  Together with them, O king, Savyasācī the scorcher of foes conquered the entire island and the king Prativindhya.

**2.23.16**  Against the inhabitants of the whole island and the kings of the seven islands, a tremendous conflict arose with Arjuna's armies.

**2.23.17**  The great archer, having conquered them too, O bull of the Bharatas, then advanced upon Prāgjyotiṣa together with all of them.

**2.23.18**  There was a great king there, Bhagadatta, O lord of the people; with him there was an immense battle with the great-souled Pāṇḍava.

**2.23.19**  Surrounded by Kirātas and Cīnas, the king of Prāgjyotiṣa stood, along with many other warriors dwelling along the ocean shores.

**2.23.20**  Then, after fighting with Dhanaṃjaya for eight days, the king, free from fatigue in battle, spoke smiling:

**2.23.21**  "It is fitting, O mighty-armed one, O joy of the Pāṇḍavas, O heir of Pākaśāsana, that such valor adorns you in battle.

**2.23.22**  I am the friend of the lord of the gods, not inferior to Indra in battle, and yet I cannot stand before you in combat, my son.

**2.23.23**  What do you desire, O son of Pāṇḍu? Speak — what shall I do for you? Whatever you say, O mighty-armed one, that I shall do, my son."

**2.23.24**  Arjuna said: "The bull of the Kurus, King Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Dharma — I desire sovereignty for him; let tribute be paid to him."

**2.23.25**  "You are a friend of my father, and are dear to me as well; therefore I do not command you — with affection, let it be given."

**2.23.26**  Bhagadatta said: "As you are like a son of Kuntī to me, so is King Yudhiṣṭhira; all of this I shall do — what else shall I do for you?"

**2.24.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having conquered him, the mighty-armed Dhanaṃjaya, son of Kuntī, proceeded northward from there, to the direction protected by Dhana-da (Kubera).

**2.24.2**  The son of Kuntī, that bull among men, conquered the inner mountain region, the outer mountain region, and likewise the region above the mountains.

**2.24.3**  Conquering all the mountain regions and the lords of men there, placing them under his sway, he took their jewels in their entirety.

**2.24.4**  Together with all of them, having won the affection of those kings, he then, O king, advanced upon Bṛhanta, the dweller in the Kulūta region.

**2.24.5**  Shaking this earth with the excellent sound of mṛdaṅga drums, the rumble of chariot wheels, and the trumpeting of elephants —

**2.24.6**  Then the young Bṛhanta, with a four-division army, sallied forth from that city and engaged the Pāṇḍava in battle.

**2.24.7**  There was an immense clash between Dhanaṃjaya and Bṛhanta, but Bṛhanta was unable to withstand the Pāṇḍava's valor.

**2.24.8**  Knowing the son of Kuntī to be most irresistible, that lord of the mountain, of poor judgment, turned back, taking all his jewels with him.

**2.24.9**  Having established that kingdom, he set out together with the Kulūtas, O king, and swiftly wrested Senābindu from his realm.

**2.24.10**  He brought together Modāpura, Vāmadeva, Sudāman, the populous Susaṃkula, the Kulūtas, the northerners, and all those kings.

**2.24.11**  Stationed there and acting by the command of the Dharma-king, Dhanaṃjaya, O king, conquered five regions in due measure.

**2.24.12**  Having reached Divaḥprastha, the great city of Senābindu, that lord encamped there with his fourfold army.

**2.24.13**  Surrounded by all of them, that great-splendoured bull among men advanced against the king Viṣvagaśva, the Paurava.

**2.24.14**  Having conquered in battle the heroic mountain-dwellers and great warriors, he conquered with his bannered army, O king, the city guarded by the Paurava.

**2.24.15**  Having thoroughly conquered the Paurava, the forest-dwelling brigands, and the seven assemblies of the Utsavasaṃketa, that Pāṇḍava prevailed.

**2.24.16**  Then that bull among warriors conquered the heroic Kāśmīra warriors along with Lohita and the ten Maṇḍalas.

**2.24.17**  Then the son of Kuntī, O king, caused the Trigartans, the Dārvas, the Kokanadas, and all the many warriors there to submit on every side.

**2.24.18**  Then the joy of the Kurus conquered the lovely Abhisārī and subjugated Rocamāna, the dweller in the Uraśā region, in battle.

**2.24.19**  Then the son of Pākaśāsana, relying on his forces, crushed the lovely Siṃhapura, well-guarded by Citrāyudha, in battle.

**2.24.20**  Then Kirīṭī, that bull among Pāṇḍavas, the joy of the Kurus, crushed the Suhmas and the Colas together with his entire army.

**2.24.21**  Then that supremely valiant joy of the Kurus brought the Bāhlīkas, hard to assail, under his sway by means of great overwhelming force.

**2.24.22**  Retaining the best of the troops and discarding the weaklings, the Pāṇḍava, son of Pākaśāsana, conquered the Daradas together with the Kāmbojas.

**2.24.23**  Those who dwell in the north-eastern quarter, those brigands, and those who live in the forest — that lord conquered them all.

**2.24.24**  The Lohas, the paramount Kāmbojas, the Ṛṣikas, and the northerners together, O great king — the son of Pākaśāsana conquered them all.

**2.24.25**  Among the Ṛṣikas, however, a most terrifying battle arose between the paramount Ṛṣika lord and Pārtha, resembling the Tārakāmaya war.

**2.24.26**  Having conquered the Ṛṣikas in the forefront of battle, O king, he brought back eight horses resembling the belly of a parrot in colour, others resembling peacocks, and yet more of both kinds.

**2.24.27**  Having thoroughly conquered the Himavat range with its forest groves in battle, that bull among men reached the Śveta mountain and encamped there.

**2.25.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: That hero, having crossed the Śveta mountain, O Bhārata, reached the land inhabited by the Kiṃpuruṣas, guarded by the son of Druma.

**2.25.2**  With a great clash of armies, that destroyer of warriors, the foremost of Pāṇḍavas, conquered and brought them to submission under tribute.

**2.25.3**  Having conquered him, the son of Pākaśāsana, undistracted, advanced with his army upon the land called Hāṭaka, guarded by the Guhyakas.

**2.25.4**  Having subdued them by conciliation, the joy of the Kurus beheld the excellent Lake Mānasa and all those rivers flowing from the sages' settlements.

**2.25.5**  Having reached Lake Mānasa, that lord, the Pāṇḍava, then conquered the land around the Hāṭaka region, guarded by the Gandharvas.

**2.25.6**  There he obtained as tribute Tittiri-spotted horses, Maṇḍūkākṣa horses, and the finest steeds from that Gandharva city.

**2.25.7**  Having reached the northern Harivarṣa, that Pāṇḍava, the delight of Pākaśāsana, wished to conquer that land.

**2.25.8**  Then the gatekeepers, of mighty frame, great valour, and immense strength, approached him joyfully and spoke these words.

**2.25.9**  "O Pārtha, this city cannot be conquered by you in any manner whatsoever. Turn back, O auspicious one — this much is enough, O Acyuta."

**2.25.10**  "Whoever enters this city will surely cease to be a mortal being. We are pleased with you, O hero; your conquest is sufficient."

**2.25.11**  "Nor is there anything here for Arjuna to conquer that can be discerned — for these are the northern Kurus, and no battle takes place here."

**2.25.12**  "Even if you were to enter, O son of Kuntī, you would see nothing here, for this place cannot be perceived by one in a human body."

**2.25.13**  "But if you wish to accomplish something else here, O tiger among men, speak it and we shall fulfil it at your word, O Bhārata."

**2.25.14**  Then Arjuna, son of Pākaśāsana, spoke to them, O king: "I wish to establish the sovereignty of the wise Dharma-king."

**2.25.15**  "I shall not enter your land, nor impose the burden of subjugation by mortals — let whatever tribute pleasing to Yudhiṣṭhira be given to us."

**2.25.16**  Then they presented him as tribute divine garments, divine ornaments, and divine skins of the Moka deer.

**2.25.17**  Thus that tiger among men conquered the northern quarter, having fought very many battles against warriors and against brigands alike.

**2.25.18**  Having thoroughly conquered those kings and placed them under tribute, he took from all of them wealth and various kinds of gems.

**2.25.19**  Horses spotted like the Tittiri bird, others resembling the wing of a parrot, others resembling peacocks — all of them swift as the wind.

**2.25.20**  Surrounded by a very great fourfold army, O king, that hero returned once more to the foremost of cities, Śakraprastha.

**2.26.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: At this very same time the valiant Bhīmasena also, having taken leave of the Dharma-king, set out towards the eastern quarter.

**2.26.2**  Accompanied by a great wheel of forces that crushed enemy kingdoms, that tiger of the Bharatas, increaser of foes' grief, marched forth.

**2.26.3**  That tiger among kings, the Pāṇḍava, going to the great city of the Pāñcālas, pacified the Pāñcālas by various means.

**2.26.4**  Then that hero, bull among men, having conquered the Gaṇḍakī and the Videhas in a short time, that lord advanced to the Daśārṇas.

**2.26.5**  There the Dāśārṇaka king Sudharmā performed a hair-raising feat — he fought a great unarmed battle with Bhīma.

**2.26.6**  But Bhīmasena, the scorcher of foes, seeing that feat of his, made the mighty Sudharmā his commander of the army.

**2.26.7**  Then Bhīma of fearful valour marched towards the eastern quarter with a great army, O king, as if shaking the earth.

**2.26.8**  He, the best of the strong, O king, conquered in battle the lord of the Aśvamedha sacrifice, Rocamāna, together with his younger brother.

**2.26.9**  Having conquered him without overly fierce action, that son of Kuntī of great heroism, the joy of the Kurus, subdued the eastern land.

**2.26.10**  Then coming southward, he brought under his sway the great city of the Pulindas and the delicate Sumitra, lord of men.

**2.26.11**  Then by the command of the Dharma-king, O Janamejaya, that bull of the Bharatas advanced against the mighty Śiśupāla.

**2.26.12**  The Cedi king too, hearing what the Pāṇḍava intended, came out from the city and received him, O scorcher of foes.

**2.26.13**  Those two meeting, O great king — the bulls of the Kuru and the Cedi — enquired about the well-being of each other's families.

**2.26.14**  Then, having offered his kingdom, the Cedi king, O lord of the people, said to Bhīma with a laugh: "What is this you are doing, O sinless one?"

**2.26.15**  Bhīma then told him of the Dharma-king's intention, and that lord of men, accepting it, acted accordingly.

**2.26.16**  Then Bhīma, O king, having stayed there thirty nights and been honoured by Śiśupāla, departed with his forces and conveyances.

**2.27.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then in the Kumāra region that subduer of foes conquered Śreṇimān and also Bṛhadbala, lord of Kosala.

**2.27.2**  At Ayodhyā, the foremost of Pāṇḍavas conquered the righteous, far-sighted, and mighty king without overly fierce action.

**2.27.3**  Then that lord conquered Gopālakaccha, the excellent and northern Mallakas, and also the king who was lord of the Mallas.

**2.27.4**  Then approaching the slopes of the Himavat and reaching Jaradgava, that strong one brought the entire region under his sway in a short time.

**2.27.5**  Having thus conquered many and various lands, that bull among men, the Pāṇḍava of very great heroism, best of the strong, also conquered all around Unnāṭa and the mountain Kukṣimanta.

**2.27.6**  The long-armed Bhīma of fearful valour brought under his sway in battle the king of Kāśī, Subandhu, who never retreated.

**2.27.7**  Then that bull among Pāṇḍavas conquered all around Supārśva and also Kratha, lord of kings, who fought with all his might in the engagement.

**2.27.8**  Then the greatly splendoured one conquered the Matsyas, the mighty and blameless Malayas, the Gayas, and the Paśubhūmi in its entirety.

**2.27.9**  The long-armed one, turning back, thoroughly conquered Madarvīka mountain with its surrounding region and marched northward; and the son of Kuntī, mighty in strength, conquered the Vatsabhūmi by force.

**2.27.10**  He also conquered the lord of the Bhārgas, the lord of the Niṣādas, and many rulers of the earth with Maṇimān at their head.

**2.27.11**  Then the Pāṇḍava Bhīma swiftly conquered the southern Mallas and the Bhogavanta without overly fierce action.

**2.27.12**  That lord conquered the Śarmakas and the Varmakas by conciliation alone; and that tiger among men conquered without overly fierce action Janaka, the Vaideha king, lord of the earth.

**2.27.13**  While stationed in the Videha land, the Pāṇḍava son of Kuntī conquered from nearby Indraparvata seven lords of the Kirātas.

**2.27.14**  Then the valiant son of Kuntī, having conquered the Suhmas, the Eastern Suhmas, and the Samakṣas in battle, that strong one advanced upon the Māgadhas.

**2.27.15**  Having conquered Daṇḍa and Daṇḍadhāra and the lords of the earth, together with all of them he advanced upon Girivraja.

**2.27.16**  Having conciliated Jārāsaṃdhi and placed him under tribute, that strong one, O king, advanced against Karṇa together with all of them.

**2.27.17**  Shaking the earth as it were with his fourfold army, that foremost of Pāṇḍavas battled with Karṇa, slayer of enemies.

**2.27.18**  Having conquered Karṇa in battle and brought him to submission, O Bhārata, that strong one then conquered the mountain-dwelling kings.

**2.27.19**  Then the Pāṇḍava struck down in the great battle the even mightier king of Modāgiri by the strength of his arms.

**2.27.20**  Then the heroic, mighty Vāsudeva, lord of the Pauṇḍras, and the king of great energy who dwelt on the bank of the Kauśikī river —

**2.27.21**  Both surrounded by forces, both heroes, both of fierce valour — having conquered those two in battle, O great king, he advanced upon the king of Vaṅga.

**2.27.22**  Having conquered Samudrasena, the king Candrasena, the king of Tāmralipta, and Kāca the lord of Vaṅga —

**2.27.23**  And the lord of the Suhmas, those who dwell by the ocean, and all the troops of Mlecchas — that bull of the Bharatas conquered them all.

**2.27.24**  Having thus conquered many and various lands and taken wealth from them, the mighty son of the Wind-god advanced to the Lauhitya river.

**2.27.25**  He made all the Mleccha kings and those dwelling on the ocean islands pay tribute and various kinds of gems.

**2.27.26**  Sandalwood, aloe, garments, incomparable gems and pearls, gold, silver, diamonds, and coral — great wealth of every kind.

**2.27.27**  That one of immeasurable soul, with hundreds of crores of that great wealth, then showered the Pandava with a rain of riches.

**2.27.28**  Then Bhima of fearsome valor, arriving at Indraprastha, reported all that wealth to the king of righteousness.

**2.28.1**  Vaishampayana said: In like manner Sahadeva too, honored by the king of righteousness, marched with a great army toward the southern direction.

**2.28.2**  That powerful lord, the Kaurava hero, first conquered the Shurasenas completely and then forcibly brought the king of the Matsyas under his sway.

**2.28.3**  He also conquered Dantavakra, lord of lords, in great battle, made him a tribute-payer, and established him securely in his own kingdom.

**2.28.4**  He brought Sukumara and King Sumitra under his control, and likewise conquered the western Matsyas and the Pataccharas.

**2.28.5**  The wise hero swiftly conquered the Nishada land, the excellent mountain Goshriṅga, and likewise King Shreṇiman.

**2.28.6**  Having fully conquered Navarashtra, he advanced against Kuntibhoja; and that king received his command with affection.

**2.28.7**  Then on the bank of the Chamravati he encountered the son of Jambhaka, a king who had been spared by Vasudeva from among former enemies.

**2.28.8**  There he engaged in battle with the Bhoja king, O Bharata; having conquered him in combat, he then marched facing south.

**2.28.9**  Having received tribute and various jewels from them, he then marched toward the Narmada accompanied by those very kings.

**2.28.10**  The valiant son of the Ashvins conquered in battle the two heroes Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti, who were surrounded by a great army.

**2.28.11**  Then, having taken their jewels, he went to the city of Mahishmati; there that bull among men engaged in battle with King Nila.

**2.28.12**  The Pandava Sahadeva, slayer of enemy heroes, a man of great valor — a very great battle then arose there, terrifying to the fainthearted.

**2.28.13**  It was a battle that caused the destruction of troops and placed lives in jeopardy; for the blessed Fire-god, the bearer of oblations, rendered his aid to that king.

**2.28.14**  Then horses, chariots, elephants, men, and coats of armor were all seen blazing with fire in Sahadeva's army.

**2.28.15**  Then the son of the Kuru line became greatly agitated in mind and was unable to offer any reply, O Janamejaya.

**2.28.16**  Janamejaya said: Why, O twice-born one, did the blessed Fire-god become an enemy in battle to Sahadeva, who was striving for the sake of the sacrifice?

**2.28.17**  Vaishampayana said: It is heard that the blessed bearer of oblations, a resident of Mahishmati, was formerly bound there as an adulterer.

**2.28.18**  He had come to be under the authority of the former kings of Nila's line, wandering there by chance in the form of a brahmin.

**2.28.19**  But the righteous king punished him according to the law; thereupon the blessed bearer of oblations blazed up in anger.

**2.28.20**  Seeing him, the king was astonished and bowed his head to the wise one; then Vibhavasu showed grace to that king.

**2.28.21**  The best of those who make excellent offerings granted a boon to that king; and the lord of the earth chose safety for his own army.

**2.28.22**  From that time forward, whatever kings out of ignorance seek to conquer that city by force, O king, they are burned by fire here in this world.

**2.28.23**  In that city of Mahishmati, O best of the Kurus, the women are said to have become unapproachable in that they follow their own desire.

**2.28.24**  Thus Fire granted a boon that women should not be restrained; indeed the women there are svairinis who move about as they please.

**2.28.25**  And kings shun that kingdom, O best of men, out of fear of Fire, O great king — so it has been from that time forward and always.

**2.28.26**  But Sahadeva of righteous soul, seeing his army terror-struck and surrounded by fire, O king, stood firm like a mountain.

**2.28.27**  Then, having touched water and become ritually pure, he addressed the Purifier: "This undertaking is for your sake, O black-pathed one — homage be to you."

**2.28.28**  "You are the mouth of the gods; you are the sacrifice itself, O Purifier. Because you purify, you are called Pavaka; because you carry, you are called Havyavahana."

**2.28.29**  "The Vedas too were born for your sake, and therefore you are called Jatavedas. It is not fitting that you obstruct this sacrifice, O carrier of oblations."

**2.28.30**  Having spoken thus, that tiger among men, the son of Madri, spread kusha grass on the ground according to ritual and sat facing the Purifier.

**2.28.31**  In the sight of the entire terrified and agitated army, O Bharata, Fire did not pass beyond him, just as the great ocean does not cross its shore.

**2.28.32**  Fire, approaching gently, spoke these words to Sahadeva, that son of the Kurus, that god among men, beginning with conciliation:

**2.28.33**  "Rise, rise up, O Kaurava! This was a test I was conducting. I know fully the intention of both you and the son of Dharma."

**2.28.34**  "But this city must be protected by me, O best of the Bharatas, so long as the lineage-holders of this king Nila endure — yet I shall fulfil the desire of your heart, O Pandava."

**2.28.35**  Then that bull among men, the son of Madri, rose up with a gladdened heart, and with joined palms and bowed head, he worshipped the Purifier.

**2.28.36**  When the Purifier withdrew, King Nila then came forward and honored with hospitality Sahadeva, that tiger among men, that lord of warriors.

**2.28.37**  Having accepted that hospitality and placed him under tribute, the son of Madri then marched victoriously toward the southern direction.

**2.28.38**  Having brought the king of Tripura under his sway and swiftly subdued the lord of Potana, that long-armed hero,

**2.28.39**  that great-armed hero, with great effort, then brought Ahriti and Kaushikācharya under his sway, and likewise the lord of Surashtra.

**2.28.40**  And while dwelling in the territory of Surashtra, he sent envoys to King Rukmini, the wise high minister stationed at Bhojakat.

**2.28.41**  To Bhishmaka himself, the righteous soul, who was a direct ally of Indra — he and his son, O king, accepted his command.

**2.28.42**  That long-armed lord of warriors, glancing affectionately toward Vasudeva, then took those jewels and marched on again.

**2.28.43**  Then that one of great splendor and immense power brought under his sway the region of Shurparaka and the clan called Upakrita, and also the Dandakas.

**2.28.44**  And the kings who dwelt on ocean islands, born of barbarian lineage, the Nishadhas, the Purushadānas, and even those who use their ears as coverings —

**2.28.45**  and those men called Kalamukhās born of Raksasa stock, and the entire mountain Kollagiri, and the city of Murachi —

**2.28.46**  and the island called Tamra, and the mountain Ramaka — the great-minded hero brought all these under his sway, along with the king Timingila.

**2.28.47**  One-footed men who dwelt solely in the forests, the city of Sanjayanti, Picchhanda, and Karahataka — these he brought under his sway merely through envoys and made them pay tribute.

**2.28.48**  The Pandyas and the Dravidians together with the Udras and Keralas, the Andhras and the Talavanas, the Kalingas and the Oshthakarnikas —

**2.28.49**  Antakhi and Roma and likewise the city of the Yavanas — these too he brought under his sway merely through envoys and made them pay tribute.

**2.28.50**  The wise subduer of enemies, that righteous soul, son of Madravati, having gone to Bharukaccha, sent envoys with affection to the great-souled Vibhishana of the Paulastya line, O king of kings.

**2.28.51**  And he accepted his command with affection; the wise lord also approved that which time had wrought.

**2.28.52**  Then Vibhishana sent various jewels, foremost among which were sandalwood and aloe, and divine ornaments as well,

**2.28.53**  and garments of great worth and gems of immense value. Then the wise and valiant Sahadeva turned back.

**2.28.54**  Having thus conquered by force, by conciliation, and by victory, and having made kings into tribute-payers, that subduer of enemies returned.

**2.28.55**  Having reported everything to the king of righteousness, O bull of the Bharatas, he who had accomplished his task dwelt in happiness, O king Janamejaya.

**2.29.1**  Vaishampayana said: I shall now tell of the deeds and victories of Nakula, and how that lord conquered the direction that Vasudeva had won.

**2.29.2**  Marching out from Khandavaprastha and heading toward the western direction, the wise one advanced with a great army.

**2.29.3**  With the great lion-roar of warriors, with their battle-shouts, and with the rumbling sound of chariot-wheels, he made this earth tremble.

**2.29.4**  Then he advanced upon Rohitaka, a beautiful and wealthy land rich in cattle, horses, and grain, beloved of Kartikeya.

**2.29.5**  There a great battle took place with the heroic Mattamayurakas; and he likewise conquered the entire desert region and Bahudhanyaka.

**2.29.6**  That one of great splendor brought Shairishaka and Mahichchha under his sway, along with the Shibis, the Trigartas, the Ambasthas, the Malavas, and the Panchakarpaṭas.

**2.29.7**  Likewise the people of Madhyamika and the Vatadhanā brahmins, and then turning back, those who dwelt in the Pushkara forest.

**2.29.8**  That bull among men conquered the clans called Utsavasanketa, and the mighty Gramaneyas who dwelt along the banks of the Sindhu.

**2.29.9**  And the Shudra and Abhira clans, those who subsist near the Sarasvati, those who live by fishing, and those who dwell in the mountains —

**2.29.10**  the entire Panchanada, and likewise the western territory, Uttarajyotika, the city of Vrindataka, and Dvarapala — all these that one of great splendor swiftly brought under his sway.

**2.29.11**  The Ramathas, the Harahunas, and whatever kings of the western regions — all of these the Pandava brought under his sway through his mere command.

**2.29.12**  While dwelling there, the mighty one sent a message to Vasudeva; and he accepted his command with ten kingdoms.

**2.29.13**  Then the powerful hero went to Shakala, the great city of the Madras, and with affection brought his maternal uncle Shalya under his sway.

**2.29.14**  Honored in turn by that king, as one deserving of honor, O lord of the people, the lord of warriors took abundant jewels and set out again.

**2.29.15**  Then he brought under his sway the exceedingly fierce barbarians dwelling in the belly of the ocean, the Pahlavas and the Barbaras, all of them.

**2.29.16**  Then, having taken jewels and having brought the kings under his sway, that best of men, Nakula, skilled in wondrous ways, turned back.

**2.29.17**  Ten thousand camels carried the treasury of that great-souled hero, O great king — immense wealth, as though with difficulty.

**2.29.18**  Then the glorious son of Madri, coming to the hero Yudhishthira who had returned to Indraprastha, presented that wealth to him.

**2.29.19**  Thus Nakula, that bull of the Bharatas, conquered the western direction, guarded by Varuna, which had previously been won by Vasudeva.

**2.30.1**  Vaishampayana said: Through the protection of the king of righteousness, through the upholding of truth, and through the destruction of enemies, the subjects were devoted to their own duties.

**2.30.2**  Through the proper collection of revenue from the powerful and through governance in accordance with righteousness, the rain-cloud showered as desired and the realm became prosperous.

**2.30.3**  All undertakings proceeded well — the tending of cattle, agriculture, and trade; all of this in particular arose from the activity of the king.

**2.30.4**  O king, from thieves or deceivers, or from one another, or even from those favoured by the king — false words were not heard.

**2.30.5**  Drought, excessive rain, epidemic, and devastation by fire — all of this was absent when Yudhiṣṭhira was devoted to dharma.

**2.30.6**  To do him honour, to attend upon him, and to present their natural tribute, kings came separately — not for any other business.

**2.30.7**  Through righteous means of acquiring wealth, his treasury grew vast — a treasury whose depletion could not be accomplished even in hundreds of years.

**2.30.8**  Having gauged the extent of his treasury and granary, the king, the son of Kuntī, directed his mind solely toward the sacrifice.

**2.30.9**  All his well-wishers, separately and together, said to him: "O lord, the time for your sacrifice has come — let it now be performed."

**2.30.10**  While they were saying this, Hari arrived — the ancient sage, the embodiment of the Vedas, visible only to those with true knowledge.

**2.30.11**  The foremost of all beings, moving and stationary, both the origin and dissolution of the world, the lord of what was, what will be, and what is — Keśava, the slayer of Keśin.

**2.30.12**  The rampart of all the Vṛṣṇis, the giver of fearlessness in times of calamity, the slayer of foes — having placed the great drum in the charge of Bala and set out with force —

**2.30.13**  Mādhava, that tiger among men, taking with him wealth of every kind — high and low — for the Dharma-king, came attended by a great army.

**2.30.14**  Resounding with the roar of his chariot, he entered the best of cities, bringing that boundless flood of wealth, that inexhaustible ocean of jewels.

**2.30.15**  As a sunless place rejoices at the arrival of the sun, as a windless space rejoices at the coming of the wind — so did the Bhārata city rejoice at the arrival of Kṛṣṇa.

**2.30.16**  Yudhiṣṭhira went joyfully to meet him, honoured him according to due rite, enquired after his welfare, and when he was comfortably seated —

**2.30.17**  That bull among men, together with Bhīma, Arjuna, and the twins, and with the officiating priests headed by Dhaumya and Dvaipāyana, spoke to Kṛṣṇa:

**2.30.18**  "O Kṛṣṇa, the entire earth has come under my sway because of you; and much wealth too, O Vārṣṇeya, has been earned through your grace.

**2.30.19**  I wish to employ all of that, O son of Devakī, according to the prescribed rites — for the foremost brāhmaṇas and in the sacrificial fire, O Mādhava.

**2.30.20**  I wish to perform the sacrifice, O Dāśārha, together with you and my younger brothers, O great-armed one — please grant me your permission.

**2.30.21**  Initiate yourself for the rite too, O Govinda, O great-armed one; for when you have sacrificed, O Dāśārha, I shall be freed of all sin.

**2.30.22**  Or grant your approval to me, together with my younger brothers, O lord; sanctioned by you, O Kṛṣṇa, I shall attain the highest sacrifice."

**2.30.23**  Kṛṣṇa replied to him at length, extolling his many virtues: "You alone, O tiger among kings, are worthy of the great imperial sacrifice. Accomplish it — when you have attained it, we shall all have fulfilled our purpose."

**2.30.24**  "Perform the sacrifice you desire, with me standing by for your welfare. Employ me too in the task — I shall do everything you command."

**2.30.25**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: "O Kṛṣṇa, my resolve has borne fruit and my success is assured — for you, O Hṛṣīkeśa, have come to me just as I desired."
